Control and Dynamics

This appendix collects the models needed to reason from a robot command to the motion it can produce. It develops state-space dynamics, feedback stability, trajectory continuity, stopping and contact bounds, and state-estimation uncertainty. Each result identifies the assumptions under which it can support a tracking or containment decision in the main text.

How to Use This Appendix

Use the table to enter at the physical question being checked. For a first encounter with control theory, start with state-space models and PID before consulting stability or constrained control. The longer Riccati, Lie-derivative, and differential-game derivations supply optional depth; the local model, assumptions, and resulting bound are the reference tools needed by the main text.

When you encounter this systems symptom Consult For this quantitative tool
An inner-loop tracking controller drifts or loses asymptotic convergence section 1.2 Continuous and discrete state-space models and Kalman controllability rank
Unmodeled gravity droop or friction causes steady-state tracking offset section 1.2.3 PID closed-loop error dissipation, Rayleigh damping, and online bias estimation
Nonlinear multi-joint couplings distort tracking across dynamic maneuvers section 1.2.4 Feedback linearization and computed torque decoupling to linear double integrators
Balancing tracking error against control effort before checking actuator limits section 1.2.5 Linear quadratic regulator (LQR), Riccati equation, and ideal full-state robustness margins
Certifying closed-loop convergence or designing stabilizing feedback for nonlinear plants section 1.3.1 Lyapunov stability theorems, domain of attraction, and Control Lyapunov Functions (CLFs)
Receding-horizon trajectory planning under hard state bounds and actuator limits section 1.3.3 Constrained Model Predictive Control (MPC) and recursive feasibility guarantees
A learned policy commands unsafe actions near physical obstacles or joint limits section 1.4 Control Barrier Function (CBF-QP) safety filters and kinetic stopping insets
Mode switching (policy handover, human intervention, or fallback) causes torque spikes section 1.5 \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) quintic splines, \(\DifferentiabilityClass^1\) cubic Hermite interpolation, and bumpless transfer bounds
Sizing safety envelopes, validity leases, or worst-case sets under adversarial disturbance section 1.6 Kinematic profiles, watchdog leases, and Hamilton-Jacobi differential games
An articulated manipulator stalls, exceeds joint velocity limits, or loses control authority section 1.6.1 Manipulator Jacobian singularities and Yoshikawa manipulability index
Spatial sensor offsets or IMU-camera lever arms corrupt state estimation under rotation section 1.6.4 Spatial coordinate transforms (\(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\)) and lever-arm acceleration propagation
Discrete sampling and transport latency erode control loop stability margins section 1.5.5 Discrete zero-order hold (ZOH) phase lag and latency margin penalties
Physics simulation diverges, impacts exceed limits, or wheels spin out during maneuvers section 1.7 Contact complementarity, elastodynamic impact, and wheel slip boundaries
A grasp slips under arm acceleration or crushes the object section 1.7.5 Coulomb friction cones, minimum squeeze force, grasp map, and force closure
Sensor occlusions or unobservable states cause state estimation uncertainty to diverge section 1.8 Continuous Lyapunov covariance propagation and Lie derivative observability rank
Teleoperation or compliant contact chatters due to transport delay section 1.10 Passivity conditions, wave variables, and energy-bounded interaction
Underactuated legged or humanoid robots balance dynamic momentum across multi-contact points section 1.9 Centroidal momentum equations and hierarchical Whole-Body Control QP

Classical and Modern State-Space Control

A robot moving toward a target retains velocity when a new command arrives. A state-space model records that memory explicitly: its state contains enough information to predict subsequent motion from the current state and future inputs. A learned policy can propose an input, but the plant dynamics determine how that input changes position, velocity, and stored energy.

The physical body is the plant, the system a controller acts on (Actuator Transmission Limits). Its state \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)\) can include joint positions, velocities, winding currents, and temperatures. These quantities are physically constrained, but they are not all conserved. Actuators change their rates of change; they do not overwrite the state instantaneously. For a mass driven by force, for example, the state contains both position and velocity because force changes acceleration before it changes either of them.

Linear and nonlinear state-space representations

State-space equations track how a plant’s internal state evolves under actuator commands and what sensors observe from that state. In the notation below, a dot denotes a time derivative, bold symbols collect coordinates into vectors or matrices, and matrix multiplication describes how one coordinate affects another. An eigenvalue describes the growth, decay, or oscillation of a linear mode; a negative real part means decay in continuous time, while discrete-time decay requires magnitude below one. A positive-definite matrix assigns a positive quadratic measure to every nonzero vector. These interpretations connect the algebra below to motion and stored energy.

A continuous-time Linear Time-Invariant (LTI) dynamic system is represented in canonical state-space form as: \[ \PlantStateRate(\TimeInstant) = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) + \StateSpaceInputMatrix \ControlInput(\TimeInstant) \] \[ \MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant) = \MeasurementMatrix \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) + \FeedthroughMatrix \ControlInput(\TimeInstant) \] where \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) \in \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension\) denotes the internal state vector (e.g., joint angles and velocities, motor winding currents, or vehicle pose and velocity), \(\ControlInput(\TimeInstant) \in \mathbb{R}^\ControlInputDimension\) is the control input vector (e.g., commanded motor currents, voltages, or torques), and \(\MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant) \in \mathbb{R}^\OutputDimension\) represents the measured output vector produced by physical sensors. The system matrix \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\StateDimension \times \StateDimension}\) governs unforced internal system dynamics (the natural drift vector field). The input matrix \(\StateSpaceInputMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\StateDimension \times \ControlInputDimension}\) models control authority over state rates. The output matrix \(\MeasurementMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\OutputDimension \times \StateDimension}\) maps internal states to sensor measurements. The feedthrough matrix \(\FeedthroughMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\OutputDimension \times \ControlInputDimension}\) represents direct feedthrough, which vanishes in strictly proper physical plants (\(\FeedthroughMatrix = \ZeroMatrix\)).

When physical dynamics include kinematic nonlinearities, centrifugal couplings, or configuration-dependent gravity (section 1.6.1), the dynamics generalize to the control-affine nonlinear form: \[ \PlantStateRate(\TimeInstant) = \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)) + \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)) \ControlInput(\TimeInstant) \] where \(\ControlAffineDriftField: \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension \to \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension\) is the unforced drift vector field and \(\ControlAffineInputField: \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension \to \mathbb{R}^{\StateDimension \times \ControlInputDimension}\) is the state-dependent control effectiveness matrix.

Discrete-time equivalent under zero-order hold

Digital microcontrollers and real-time operating systems execute periodic control loops at sampling frequency \(\SamplingRate = 1 / \SamplingPeriod\) (e.g., \(1\text{ kHz}\), \(\SamplingPeriod = 1\text{ ms}\); see Multi-Rate Cadences). When the actuator driver holds control inputs constant across each clock tick using a Zero-Order Hold (\(\ControlInput(\TimeInstant) = \ControlInput_k\) for \(\TimeInstant \in [\ControlSampleIndex \SamplingPeriod, (\ControlSampleIndex+1) \SamplingPeriod)\)), the continuous LTI dynamics integrate into the exact discrete-time recurrence: \[ \MechanicalPlantState_{k+1} = \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix \MechanicalPlantState_k + \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix \ControlInput_k \] where the discrete transition operators evaluate via the continuous matrix exponential: \[ \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix = e^{\StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod} = \IdentityMatrix + \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod + \frac{1}{2!} \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^2 \SamplingPeriod^2 + \dots \] \[ \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix = \int_0^{\SamplingPeriod} e^{\StateSpaceSystemMatrix \IntegrationDummyTime} d\IntegrationDummyTime \, \StateSpaceInputMatrix = \left( \IdentityMatrix \SamplingPeriod + \frac{1}{2!} \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod^2 + \dots \right) \StateSpaceInputMatrix \] When \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\) is non-singular, the discrete input matrix simplifies to \(\DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^{-1}(e^{\StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod} - \IdentityMatrix)\StateSpaceInputMatrix\). This recurrence is exact for the stated LTI model with a held input. A nonlinear plant generally requires numerical integration or a local linear model; its discretization error must enter the prediction bound.

Discretization pathologies: Why forward Euler destabilizes physical models

To an ML engineer, a forward Euler discretization \(\MechanicalPlantState_{k+1} \approx (\IdentityMatrix + \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod)\MechanicalPlantState_k + \StateSpaceInputMatrix \SamplingPeriod \ControlInput_k = \MechanicalPlantState_k + \SamplingPeriod (\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\MechanicalPlantState_k + \StateSpaceInputMatrix\ControlInput_k)\) appears structurally identical to a Residual Network (ResNet) skip connection \(\MechanicalPlantState_{k+1} = \MechanicalPlantState_k + \ResidualFunction(\MechanicalPlantState_k)\). However, in physical dynamical systems, Euler truncation introduces spurious numerical energy that can artificially destabilize the model or ruin gradient propagation in backpropagation through time.

Consider an undamped harmonic physical oscillator \(\OscillatorAcceleration + \OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \OscillatorPosition = 0\) with state \(\MechanicalPlantState = [\OscillatorPosition, \OscillatorVelocity]^\top\) and continuous system matrix \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -\OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 & 0 \end{bmatrix}\). The continuous eigenvalues are purely imaginary: \(\MatrixEigenvalue = \pm \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency\). Analyzing how common numerical discretization schemes map these eigenvalues to the complex \(\ZTransformVariable\)-plane illustrates their stability properties:

  1. Exact Zero-Order Hold (Matrix Exponential): The discrete transition operator is \(\DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix = e^{\StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod}\). Its eigenvalues lie on the unit circle: \[ |\MatrixEigenvalue(\DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix)| = |e^{\pm \ImaginaryUnit \OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod}| = 1 \] This preserves the physical total energy \(\MechanicalEnergy = \frac{1}{2}\OscillatorVelocity^2 + \frac{1}{2}\OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \OscillatorPosition^2\) over infinite horizons without artificial growth or decay. Phase rotates at the true angular rate \(\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod\) per step.
  2. Forward Euler Discretization: Approximating the transition as \(\ForwardEulerTransitionMatrix = \IdentityMatrix + \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \SamplingPeriod \\ -\OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod & 1 \end{bmatrix}\) yields eigenvalues \(\ForwardEulerEigenvalue = 1 \pm \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod\). Their complex modulus is: \[ |\ForwardEulerEigenvalue| = \sqrt{1 + \OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod^2} > 1 \quad \forall \SamplingPeriod > 0 \] Because the spectral radius strictly exceeds unity, forward Euler simulation injects artificial numerical energy at every step, converting an intrinsically conservative physical oscillator into an exponentially diverging explosion. In differentiable simulation and policy learning, this causes exploding gradients during backpropagation through time.
  3. Backward (Implicit) Euler Discretization: Approximating transitions via \(\MechanicalPlantState_{k+1} = \MechanicalPlantState_k + \SamplingPeriod \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \MechanicalPlantState_{k+1}\) yields \(\BackwardEulerTransitionMatrix = (\IdentityMatrix - \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SamplingPeriod)^{-1}\). The discrete eigenvalues evaluate to \(\BackwardEulerEigenvalue = \frac{1}{1 \mp \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod}\), with modulus: \[ |\BackwardEulerEigenvalue| = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + \OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod^2}} < 1 \quad \forall \SamplingPeriod > 0 \] While unconditionally stable, backward Euler introduces artificial numerical dissipation (numerical damping) that bleeds energy from the physical system. In unrolled optimization, that artificial contraction causes gradient vanishing over long horizons.
  4. Bilinear (Tustin / Crank-Nicolson) Discretization: Employing the trapezoidal integration operator \(\LaplaceVariable \approx \frac{2}{\SamplingPeriod}\frac{\ZTransformVariable-1}{\ZTransformVariable+1}\) yields: \[ \TustinTransitionMatrix = \left(\IdentityMatrix - \frac{\SamplingPeriod}{2}\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\right)^{-1} \left(\IdentityMatrix + \frac{\SamplingPeriod}{2}\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\right) \] For purely imaginary continuous poles \(\LaplaceVariable = \pm \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency\), the discrete eigenvalues evaluate to \(\TustinEigenvalue = \frac{1 \pm \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod / 2}{1 \mp \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod / 2}\). Their modulus is identically unity: \[ |\TustinEigenvalue| = \left| \frac{1 \pm \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod / 2}{1 \mp \ImaginaryUnit\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod / 2} \right| = \frac{\sqrt{1 + (\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod / 2)^2}}{\sqrt{1 + (\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod / 2)^2}} = 1 \quad \forall \SamplingPeriod > 0 \] The bilinear transform maps the continuous imaginary axis directly onto the discrete unit circle, preserving energy conservation without matrix exponentials. Higher frequencies undergo frequency warping, however, because \(\arctan(\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod / 2)\) compresses the phase advance per step.
  5. Semi-Implicit (Symplectic) Euler Discretization: Staggering state updates by updating velocity with current acceleration and then updating position with the updated velocity: \[ \OscillatorStepVelocity_{k+1} = \OscillatorStepVelocity_k - \OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod \OscillatorPosition_k, \quad \OscillatorPosition_{k+1} = \OscillatorPosition_k + \SamplingPeriod \OscillatorStepVelocity_{k+1} = (1 - \OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod^2) \OscillatorPosition_k + \SamplingPeriod \OscillatorStepVelocity_k \] yields the discrete transition matrix: \[ \SymplecticEulerTransitionMatrix = \begin{bmatrix} 1 - \OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod^2 & \SamplingPeriod \\ -\OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod & 1 \end{bmatrix} \] The determinant evaluates to \(\det(\SymplecticEulerTransitionMatrix) = (1 - \OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod^2)(1) - (\SamplingPeriod)(-\OscillatorNaturalFrequency^2 \SamplingPeriod) = 1\) identically for all \(\SamplingPeriod > 0\). For this oscillator, the method is symplectic and preserves phase-space area. It conserves a modified quadratic energy rather than the original energy exactly; determinant one alone does not establish symplecticity in higher dimensions. For stable step sizes (\(\OscillatorNaturalFrequency \SamplingPeriod < 2\)), physical energy oscillates within bounded limits over infinite horizons rather than exploding or vanishing. The bounded energy error makes this method useful for conservative mechanical models, although contact, damping, and solver choices require separate analysis.

The integrator and step size must preserve the behavior needed by the application. Forward Euler can be adequate for sufficiently resolved dissipative dynamics; this undamped oscillator shows why a small step alone does not guarantee long-horizon energy fidelity. Sampling adds a separate phase effect (section 1.5.5).

Controllability and the Kalman rank condition

In deep learning, model capacity measures whether a neural architecture possesses the expressivity to represent a desired target function class. In physical systems, controllability addresses a more basic structural question, namely whether the physical actuator allocation \(\StateSpaceInputMatrix\) provides sufficient authority to steer the plant’s internal state \(\MechanicalPlantState\) to an arbitrary target configuration across the continuous dynamic transition graph \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\).

An LTI system \((\StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \StateSpaceInputMatrix)\) is controllable if, for any initial state \(\MechanicalPlantState(0) = \MechanicalPlantState_0\) and any desired target state \(\TargetPlantState\), there exists a finite time \(\SteeringTransferTime > 0\) and an unconstrained input trajectory \(\ControlInput(\TimeInstant)\) that transfers the state from \(\MechanicalPlantState_0\) to \(\TargetPlantState\). This test does not impose actuator, state, or path limits. Under that unconstrained LTI model, the condition holds if and only if the controllability matrix \(\ControllabilityMatrix\) has full row rank: \[ \ControllabilityMatrix = \begin{bmatrix} \StateSpaceInputMatrix & \StateSpaceSystemMatrix\StateSpaceInputMatrix & \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^2\StateSpaceInputMatrix & \dots & \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^{n-1}\StateSpaceInputMatrix \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{\StateDimension \times \StateDimension \ControlInputDimension}, \quad \operatorname{rank}(\ControllabilityMatrix) = \StateDimension \] Each column block \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^k \StateSpaceInputMatrix\) represents the directional reach of the control input after \(\SystemMatrixPowerIndex\) steps of dynamic diffusion through the unforced system matrix \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\). The column space \(\operatorname{im}(\ControllabilityMatrix)\) defines the controllable subspace, mathematically equivalent to the \(\StateDimension\)-th order Krylov subspace \(\KrylovSubspace_n(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \operatorname{im}(\StateSpaceInputMatrix)) = \operatorname{span}\{\StateSpaceInputMatrix, \StateSpaceSystemMatrix\StateSpaceInputMatrix, \dots, \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^{n-1}\StateSpaceInputMatrix\}\).

The matrix sequence terminates at power \(\StateDimension-1\) because the Cayley-Hamilton theorem states that every square matrix \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\StateDimension \times \StateDimension}\) satisfies its own characteristic polynomial \(\CharacteristicPolynomial(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix)=\ZeroMatrix\), where: \[ \CharacteristicPolynomial(\MatrixEigenvalue) = \det(\MatrixEigenvalue \IdentityMatrix - \StateSpaceSystemMatrix) = \MatrixEigenvalue^\StateDimension + \CharacteristicPolynomialCoefficient_{n-1} \MatrixEigenvalue^{\StateDimension-1} + \dots + \CharacteristicPolynomialCoefficient_1 \MatrixEigenvalue + \CharacteristicPolynomialCoefficient_0 \] Substituting \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\) into its polynomial yields: \[ \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^n = -\CharacteristicPolynomialCoefficient_{n-1} \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^{n-1} - \CharacteristicPolynomialCoefficient_{n-2} \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^{n-2} - \dots - \CharacteristicPolynomialCoefficient_1 \StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \CharacteristicPolynomialCoefficient_0 \IdentityMatrix \] Multiplying repeatedly by \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\) demonstrates that every higher power \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^k\) for \(\SystemMatrixPowerIndex \ge \StateDimension\) can be expressed as a linear combination of the lower powers \(\{\IdentityMatrix, \StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \dots, \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^{n-1}\}\). Consequently, adding higher powers \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^k \StateSpaceInputMatrix\) for \(\SystemMatrixPowerIndex \ge \StateDimension\) cannot increase the dimension of the subspace spanned by the columns of \(\ControllabilityMatrix\).

If \(\operatorname{rank}(\ControllabilityMatrix) < \StateDimension\), the state space decomposes into a controllable subspace and an uncontrollable component in suitable coordinates. The controllability matrix \(\ControllabilityMatrix\) evaluates whether control inputs can reach all \(\StateDimension\) state dimensions through powers of \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\). It is exactly dual to the observability matrix \(\ObservabilityMatrix\) (section 1.8), which evaluates whether sensor outputs can distinguish all \(\StateDimension\) state dimensions.

The Popov-Belevitch-Hautus (PBH) test provides an equivalent spectral condition: the pair \((\StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \StateSpaceInputMatrix)\) is controllable if and only if: \[ \operatorname{rank}\left( \begin{bmatrix} \MatrixEigenvalue \IdentityMatrix - \StateSpaceSystemMatrix & \StateSpaceInputMatrix \end{bmatrix} \right) = \StateDimension \quad \forall \MatrixEigenvalue \in \mathbb{C} \] If \(\operatorname{rank}([\MatrixEigenvalue \IdentityMatrix - \StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \StateSpaceInputMatrix]) < \StateDimension\) for some eigenvalue \(\MatrixEigenvalue\), the dynamic mode associated with \(\MatrixEigenvalue\) cannot be excited or influenced by any control input. If that uncontrollable mode is unstable (\(\operatorname{Re}(\MatrixEigenvalue) \ge 0\)), the physical plant is un-stabilizable, an unyielding physical limitation that no learned policy, foundation model, or reinforcement learning algorithm can overcome. Underactuation alone does not imply uncontrollability. Dynamic coupling can make an indirectly actuated state controllable; floating-base systems additionally depend on the available contacts (section 1.9).

Proportional-integral-derivative control as closed-loop error dissipation

When an arm link sags \(4\text{ mm}\) below its target height under a \(3\text{ kg}\) payload, position feedback pulls the joint toward the target, velocity feedback bleeds off overshoot, and accumulated-error feedback builds the extra torque needed to hold the load at zero steady-state offset. A joint-level Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID) controller combines position error, accumulated error, and error rate to generate effort. For a simple mechanical plant, these terms act as a restoring spring, bias compensation, and damping. This interpretation explains their roles; PID is not generally an optimal controller, and positive gains alone do not guarantee stability.

In this subsection, let \(\MechanicalPlantState^*(\TimeInstant)\) and \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)\) denote desired and measured joint positions, rather than the full position-velocity state. Their difference defines the instantaneous tracking error: \[ \TrackingErrorVector(\TimeInstant) = \MechanicalPlantState^*(\TimeInstant) - \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) \] The continuous PID control law synthesizes commanded control effort \(\ControlInput(\TimeInstant)\) (e.g., motor drive current, electromagnetic torque, or hydraulic valve pressure) as: \[ \ControlInput(\TimeInstant) = \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector(\TimeInstant) + \IntegralGainMatrix \int_0^\TimeInstant \TrackingErrorVector(\IntegrationDummyTime) \, d\IntegrationDummyTime + \DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate(\TimeInstant) \] where \(\ProportionalGainMatrix, \IntegralGainMatrix, \DerivativeGainMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\JointCount \times \JointCount}\) are symmetric, positive-definite gain matrices. For the energy argument below, assume a fixed setpoint, a constant scalar inertia \(\Mass>0\), and no saturation. Each term has a distinct role:

  1. Proportional Term (\(\ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector\)): Conservative Restoring Force / Gradient Descent: Consider an artificial quadratic potential loss landscape \(\ProportionalPotentialEnergy(\TrackingErrorVector) = \frac{1}{2} \TrackingErrorVector^\top \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector\). Because \(\TrackingErrorVector = \MechanicalPlantState^* - \MechanicalPlantState\), the restoring force is the negative gradient with respect to position, \(-\nabla_{\MechanicalPlantState}\ProportionalPotentialEnergy = \ProportionalGainMatrix\TrackingErrorVector\). Proportional feedback behaves as an elastic spring or continuous negative gradient step that pulls the physical coordinates toward the reference setpoint. However, on an undamped second-order plant (\(\Mass \PlantStateAcceleration = \ControlInput\)), proportional feedback alone generates an undamped harmonic oscillator \(\Mass \TrackingErrorAcceleration + \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector = \ZeroMatrix\). The system conserves total mechanical energy \(\MechanicalEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \Mass \|\TrackingErrorRate\|^2 + \frac{1}{2} \TrackingErrorVector^\top \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector\), orbiting closed level curves indefinitely with zero asymptotic convergence.
  2. Derivative Term (\(\DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate\)): Rayleigh Dissipation / Momentum Damping: To force convergence to the origin, kinetic energy must be extracted from the closed loop. The derivative term introduces an artificial viscous damper or Rayleigh dissipation function \(\RayleighDissipationFunction(\TrackingErrorRate) = \frac{1}{2} \TrackingErrorRate^\top \DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate\). Differentiating the closed-loop mechanical energy \(\MechanicalEnergy(\TrackingErrorVector, \TrackingErrorRate)\) along plant trajectories under PD feedback yields: \[ \MechanicalEnergyDerivative = \Mass \TrackingErrorRate^\top \TrackingErrorAcceleration + \TrackingErrorRate^\top \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector = \TrackingErrorRate^\top \left( -\ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector - \DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate \right) + \TrackingErrorRate^\top \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector = -\TrackingErrorRate^\top \DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate \le 0 \] Because \(\DerivativeGainMatrix \succ \ZeroMatrix\), the derivative term converts tracking kinetic energy into continuous dissipation, damping oscillations and, for this plant, steering closed-loop poles into the open left-half complex plane. In machine learning optimization, this is analogous to the friction term in the continuous heavy-ball model used to stabilize gradient oscillations across steep ravines.
  3. Integral Term (\(\IntegralGainMatrix \int_0^\TimeInstant \TrackingErrorVector(\IntegrationDummyTime) \, d\IntegrationDummyTime\)): Online Disturbance / Unmodeled Bias Estimation: In physical deployments, constant external loads \(\ExternalDisturbanceLoad\), such as gravitational sagging on robot arm links, cable friction, or payload offsets, induce a steady-state standoff. At rest (\(\TrackingErrorRate = \ZeroMatrix, \TrackingErrorAcceleration = \ZeroMatrix\)), a PD controller yields \(\ProportionalGainMatrix \SteadyStateTrackingError + \ExternalDisturbanceLoad = \ZeroMatrix\), leaving a persistent tracking error \(\SteadyStateTrackingError = -\ProportionalGainMatrix^{-1} \ExternalDisturbanceLoad\). The integral term introduces an internal dynamic state variable: \[ \IntegratorStateRate(\TimeInstant) = \TrackingErrorVector(\TimeInstant), \quad \IntegralControlEffort(\TimeInstant) = \IntegralGainMatrix \IntegratorState(\TimeInstant) \] Accumulating tracking error over time forces \(\IntegratorState(\TimeInstant)\) to integrate until \(\IntegralGainMatrix \SteadyStateIntegratorState = -\ExternalDisturbanceLoad\), counteracting the disturbance and driving steady-state position error to zero (\(\SteadyStateTrackingError \to \ZeroMatrix\)). In optimization terms, the integral term acts as an online running bias estimator that dynamically learns and cancels constant model mismatch.

For a scalar single-degree-of-freedom mechanical plant with mass \(\Mass\), the closed-loop error equation under PD control evaluates to: \[ \ScalarTrackingErrorAcceleration(\TimeInstant) + 2\DampingRatio \UndampedNaturalFrequency \ScalarTrackingErrorRate(\TimeInstant) + \UndampedNaturalFrequency^2 \ScalarTrackingError(\TimeInstant) = 0 \] where the undamped natural frequency is \(\UndampedNaturalFrequency = \sqrt{\PidProportionalGain / \Mass}\) and the dimensionless damping ratio is \(\DampingRatio = \frac{\PidDerivativeGain}{2 \sqrt{\Mass \PidProportionalGain}}\). The characteristic roots \(\LaplaceVariable_{1,2} = -\DampingRatio \UndampedNaturalFrequency \pm \UndampedNaturalFrequency \sqrt{\DampingRatio^2 - 1}\) partition the closed-loop tracking response into three physical regimes:

  • Underdamped (\(0 < \DampingRatio < 1\)): Complex conjugate poles; fast initial rise time but decaying ringing oscillations and overshoot that can damage mechanical gearboxes.
  • Critically Damped (\(\DampingRatio = 1\)): Repeated real negative root \(\LaplaceVariable = -\UndampedNaturalFrequency\); for fixed \(\UndampedNaturalFrequency\), gives the fastest nonoscillatory step response from rest in this second-order model.
  • Overdamped (\(\DampingRatio > 1\)): Distinct negative real roots; gives a slower nonoscillatory step response from rest. Other initial velocities can still produce a setpoint crossing.

Practical firmware implementation: Anti-windup and derivative filtering

While textbook PID assumes infinite actuator authority and continuous differentiation, deploying PID loops on physical robot joints exposes two cyber-physical pathologies:

  1. Derivative Kick and Noise Amplification: In textbook PID, the derivative term evaluates error rate \(\DerivativeControlEffort = \DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate = \DerivativeGainMatrix (\JointVelocitySetpoint - \GeneralizedVelocities)\). When a learned policy or planner issues a step setpoint or hands over between action chunks (Transfer Without Discontinuity), the sudden change in reference setpoint \(\JointPositionSetpoint\) creates an impulsive derivative spike \(\JointVelocitySetpoint \approx \infty\) (derivative kick). The spike commands explosive actuator torques and can trigger current limiting or an inverter fault. Furthermore, discrete differentiation of quantized encoder signals amplifies high-frequency measurement noise as \(\mathcal{O}(\AngularFrequency)\). Production firmware resolves this by differentiating solely on the measured plant state (\(\DerivativeControlEffort = -\DerivativeGainMatrix \GeneralizedVelocities\)) and routing the velocity through a first-order low-pass filter: \[ \DerivativeControlEffort(\LaplaceVariable) = -\frac{\DerivativeGainMatrix \LaplaceVariable}{\DerivativeFilterTimeConstant \LaplaceVariable + 1} \GeneralizedCoordinates(\LaplaceVariable) \] where \(\DerivativeFilterTimeConstant > 0\) sets the derivative filter roll-off frequency, preserving damping in the control band while limiting the high-frequency gain that an ideal differentiator would amplify without bound.
  2. Actuator Saturation and Integral Windup: Physical actuators are strictly bounded by torque and thermal current limits (\(\JointTorqueLowerLimit \le \JointTorque \le \JointTorqueUpperLimit\)). When a large disturbance or aggressive maneuver pushes commanded torque past physical limits, the physical plant cannot accelerate fast enough to eliminate tracking error. Meanwhile, the integral term continues accumulating error unhindered, winding up the internal dynamic state \(\IntegratorState(\TimeInstant)\) to excessive values. Once the plant finally reaches the setpoint, the stored integrator charge forces the actuator to remain saturated in the forward direction, causing violent overshoot, severe limit-cycle hunting, and structural collisions. Real-time motor firmware implements anti-windup circuitry: clamping the integrator state whenever actuator limits are reached, or employing back-calculation: \[ \IntegratorStateRate(\TimeInstant) = \TrackingErrorVector(\TimeInstant) + \frac{1}{\AntiWindupTrackingTimeConstant} \IntegralGainMatrix^{-1} \left( \SaturatedJointTorque(\TimeInstant) - \PresaturationTorqueCommand(\TimeInstant) \right) \] where \(\ControlInput=\JointTorque\), the invertible integral gain converts the torque mismatch back to error-integral units, \(\AntiWindupTrackingTimeConstant > 0\) is a tracking time constant and \(\SaturatedJointTorque = \operatorname{clip}(\PresaturationTorqueCommand, \JointTorqueLowerLimit, \JointTorqueUpperLimit)\). When the actuator is unsaturated (\(\SaturatedJointTorque = \PresaturationTorqueCommand\)), standard integration proceeds. When the actuator is saturated, the mismatch feeds back to discharge the integrator dynamically, reducing the excess stored integral command upon desaturation. With integral action, stability must be checked again: for the scalar mass model, positive gains must also satisfy \(\PidDerivativeGain \PidProportionalGain>\Mass \PidIntegralGain\).

Feedback linearization: Transforming nonlinear dynamics into decoupled double integrators

Swinging a manipulator’s elbow outward changes the shoulder’s effective inertia and injects centrifugal torque into the shoulder joint, so fixed linear gains tuned with the arm tucked become sluggish or oscillatory when the arm extends. Multi-body robotic plants are governed by coupled, configuration-dependent equations of motion (Actuator Transmission Limits): \[ \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \JointTorque \] where \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \in \mathbb{R}^{\JointCount \times \JointCount}\) is the configuration-dependent mass matrix, \(\CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities\) collects velocity-dependent generalized forces, \(\GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) is gravity, and \(\JointTorque \in \mathbb{R}^\JointCount\) is the joint torque vector.

To cancel those configuration-dependent couplings, feedback linearization (implemented here as computed torque control) uses a model of inertia, velocity coupling, and gravity to turn a requested joint acceleration into torque. The cancellation below assumes a fully actuated rigid-link model, valid joint coordinates, exact parameters, and sufficient actuator authority. It does not apply unchanged to a floating base or to saturated drives.

Because the physical inertia matrix \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) is symmetric and strictly positive definite everywhere on the configuration space (\(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \succ \ZeroMatrix\)), it is globally invertible. We define the nonlinear state-feedback control law: \[ \JointTorque = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \VirtualAccelerationCommand + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \] where \(\VirtualAccelerationCommand \in \mathbb{R}^\JointCount\) is a synthetic, virtual acceleration command. Substituting this control law directly into the physical equations of motion yields: \[ \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \VirtualAccelerationCommand + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \] Subtracting the identical Coriolis and gravity vectors from both sides isolates: \[ \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \left( \GeneralizedAccelerations - \VirtualAccelerationCommand \right) = \ZeroMatrix \] Because \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) is non-singular, multiplying by \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^{-1}\) collapses the coupled nonlinear plant into \(\JointCount\) decoupled, linear double-integrator channels: \[ \GeneralizedAccelerations = \VirtualAccelerationCommand \iff \JointAcceleration_i = \JointAcceleration_i^* \quad \forall \JointIndex \in \{1, \dots, \JointCount\} \] Over these decoupled channels, linear tracking control laws synthesize virtual acceleration commands with global exponential stability: \[ \VirtualAccelerationCommand = \ReferenceJointAccelerations + \ProportionalGainMatrix (\ReferenceJointPositions - \GeneralizedCoordinates) + \DerivativeGainMatrix (\ReferenceJointVelocities - \GeneralizedVelocities) \] Substituting \(\VirtualAccelerationCommand\) back into the double-integrator dynamics reveals that the tracking error \(\TrackingErrorVector(\TimeInstant) = \ReferenceJointPositions(\TimeInstant) - \GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant)\) satisfies the linear error ODE: \[ \TrackingErrorAcceleration + \DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate + \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector = \ZeroMatrix \] Choosing positive-diagonal gain matrices \(\ProportionalGainMatrix\) and \(\DerivativeGainMatrix\) guarantees that tracking errors in every joint coordinate decouple and decay to zero independently at the designer-specified rate. Each joint then behaves as an independent second-order oscillator whose natural frequency and damping ratio follow directly from its diagonal gain pair.

Lie-derivative formulation for general control-affine systems

A general control-affine nonlinear dynamical system has a state rate \(\PlantStateRate\) that is a drift field \(\ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\) plus an input matrix field applied linearly to \(\ControlInput\), and an output \(\MeasurementVector = \NonlinearOutputFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\). For such systems, feedback linearization differentiates each output channel \(\OutputChannel_{\OutputIndex}\) with respect to continuous time until the control input \(\ControlInput\) appears explicitly: \[ \OutputChannel_{\OutputIndex}^{(\OutputRelativeDegree_{\OutputIndex})} = \DriftLieDerivativeOperator^{\OutputRelativeDegree_{\OutputIndex}} \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputIndex}(\MechanicalPlantState) + \InputLieDerivativeOperator \DriftLieDerivativeOperator^{\OutputRelativeDegree_{\OutputIndex}-1} \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputIndex}(\MechanicalPlantState) \ControlInput \] where \(\OutputRelativeDegree_{\OutputIndex}\) is the relative degree of output \(\OutputIndex\). Collecting all output channels into vector form: \[ \MeasurementVector^{(\OutputRelativeDegree)} = \DecouplingDriftVector(\MechanicalPlantState) + \DecouplingMatrix(\MechanicalPlantState) \ControlInput \] where \(\DecouplingMatrix(\MechanicalPlantState) \in \mathbb{R}^{\OutputDimension \times \ControlInputDimension}\) is the decoupling matrix whose entries are Lie derivatives \(\DecouplingMatrixElement_{\OutputIndex, j}(\MechanicalPlantState) = \InputColumnLieDerivativeOperator \DriftLieDerivativeOperator^{\OutputRelativeDegree_{\OutputIndex}-1} \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputIndex}(\MechanicalPlantState)\). If \(\DecouplingMatrix(\MechanicalPlantState)\) is square (\(\OutputDimension = \ControlInputDimension\)) and non-singular across the operating envelope, the state-feedback law: \[ \ControlInput = \DecouplingMatrix(\MechanicalPlantState)^{-1} \left( \LinearizedVirtualInput - \DecouplingDriftVector(\MechanicalPlantState) \right) \] cancels the modeled nonlinearities in the output equations, yielding \(\MeasurementVector^{(\OutputRelativeDegree)} = \LinearizedVirtualInput\). Here \(\DriftLieDerivativeOperator \OutputComponentFunction=\nabla \OutputComponentFunction^\top \ControlAffineDriftField\) is the rate of change of \(\OutputComponentFunction\) along \(\ControlAffineDriftField\), and repeated Lie derivatives differentiate that rate again. If \(\sum_{\OutputIndex} \OutputRelativeDegree_{\OutputIndex}\) is less than the dimension of \(\MechanicalPlantState\), internal states remain outside this input-output description; their zero dynamics must also be stable.

Unlike deep learning architectures where model inaccuracies are accommodated by statistical loss minimization, computed torque control assumes perfect knowledge of physical parameters (\(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix, \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix, \GravitationalTorqueVector\)). In real hardware, parametric inaccuracies (uncertain link masses, payload variations) leave residual nonlinear perturbations \(\ResidualDynamicsPerturbation = (\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix - \EstimatedInertiaMatrix)\GeneralizedAccelerations + (\CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix - \EstimatedCoriolisMatrix)\GeneralizedVelocities + (\GravitationalTorqueVector - \EstimatedGravitationalTorque)\). Physical AI architectures resolve this by pairing feedback linearization with robust inner loops (sliding-mode control or \(\Glyph{H_\infty}\) disturbance observers) or online adaptive parameter estimators.

In modern physical AI architectures, computed torque control also provides an analytical, differentiable physics inductive bias for learned policies. Rather than forcing a high-capacity neural network to learn the entire multi-body dynamics from scratch, the system splits torque synthesis into a nominal analytical dynamics model and a learned residual policy: \[ \JointTorque = \EstimatedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \VirtualAccelerationCommand + \EstimatedCoriolisMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \EstimatedGravitationalTorque(\GeneralizedCoordinates) + \ResidualPolicyTorque(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities, \VirtualAccelerationCommand) \] The baseline physics model cancels the dominant, known multi-body nonlinearities, while the neural network \(\ResidualPolicyTorque\) is trained via reinforcement learning or imitation learning to capture unmodeled physical effects: joint cable friction, transmission backlash, hydrodynamic drag, and payload variations. This hybrid formulation can reduce the dynamics a policy must learn, but does not preserve baseline stability automatically. The residual needs an amplitude or energy bound compatible with the nominal controller, and the combined command must satisfy actuator limits.

Linear quadratic regulator and algebraic Riccati equations

In reinforcement learning (The latch task as a reward problem), policy optimization searches for an action policy \(\Policy(\RobotAction \mid \DecisionProcessState)\) that maximizes expected cumulative reward via trial-and-error sampling or learned critic networks \(\CriticReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState)\) / \(\CriticActionReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \RobotAction)\). This search frequently struggles with high gradient variance, non-convex loss landscapes, and empirical stability failures. In contrast, for linear-quadratic physical dynamics, the Linear Quadratic Regulator (LQR) provides the exact, analytical solution to the continuous-state Bellman optimality equation over an infinite time horizon, as summarized against reinforcement learning below:

Dimension Reinforcement Learning (Actor-Critic / Policy Gradient) Linear Quadratic Regulator (LQR)
Value Function (Critic) Parameterized deep neural network \(\CriticReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState)\) or \(\CriticActionReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \RobotAction)\) Exact quadratic potential \(\OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState) = \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState\)
Policy (Actor) Parameterized neural network \(\RobotAction \sim \Policy(\cdot \mid \DecisionProcessState)\) Optimal linear state feedback \(\ControlInput^* = -\LqrFeedbackGain\MechanicalPlantState\)
Bellman Optimality Dynamic programming fixed point: \(\OptimalStateReturn^*(\DecisionProcessState) = \max_{\RobotAction} [\Reward(\DecisionProcessState,\RobotAction) + \DiscountFactor \mathbb{E}[\OptimalStateReturn^*(\NextDecisionProcessState)]]\) Continuous Algebraic Riccati Equation (ARE fixed point)
Solution Method Stochastic gradient descent (PPO, SAC), TD learning Direct algebraic factorizations (Schur decomposition, ARE solvers)
Stability Guarantees Not supplied by empirical reward optimization alone Stable under the stated model and rank conditions; ideal full-state margins below
Sample Complexity Requires task- and algorithm-dependent training data No policy-training samples once \((\StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \StateSpaceInputMatrix, \LqrStateWeightMatrix, \LqrControlWeightMatrix)\) are specified; identifying the model still needs evidence

Given the controllable continuous LTI system \(\PlantStateRate = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix\MechanicalPlantState + \StateSpaceInputMatrix\ControlInput\), the infinite-horizon regulator minimizes the quadratic performance objective: \[ \LqrQuadraticObjective = \int_0^\infty \left( \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)^\top \LqrStateWeightMatrix \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) + \ControlInput(\TimeInstant)^\top \LqrControlWeightMatrix \ControlInput(\TimeInstant) \right) d\TimeInstant \] Here the two weighting matrices price state error against actuator effort:

  • \(\LqrStateWeightMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\StateDimension \times \StateDimension}\) is a symmetric positive semi-definite state penalty matrix (\(\LqrStateWeightMatrix \succeq \ZeroMatrix\)), penalizing deviations from the target state \(\MechanicalPlantState^* = \ZeroMatrix\).
  • \(\LqrControlWeightMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\ControlInputDimension \times \ControlInputDimension}\) is a symmetric positive definite control penalty matrix (\(\LqrControlWeightMatrix \succ \ZeroMatrix\)), penalizing control effort.

The weights express a trade-off, not hard physical limits. Increasing \(\LqrControlWeightMatrix\) discourages effort, while increasing \(\LqrStateWeightMatrix\) discourages state error. Neither guarantees a current ceiling, a torque slew rate, or a position bound. Those requirements need explicit constraints, such as the MPC formulation below, or a separate admissibility check (Actuator Transmission Limits).

Dynamic programming derivation of continuous LQR

In continuous time, Bellman’s principle of optimality (Bellman 1957; Åström and Murray 2021) states that the optimal cost-to-go from state \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)\) across an infinitesimal time step \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant \to 0\) satisfies: \[ \OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)) = \min_{\ControlInputWindow} \left\{ \int_t^{\TimeInstant+\FiniteChange \TimeInstant} \left( \MechanicalPlantState(\IntegrationDummyTime)^\top \LqrStateWeightMatrix \MechanicalPlantState(\IntegrationDummyTime) + \ControlInput(\IntegrationDummyTime)^\top \LqrControlWeightMatrix \ControlInput(\IntegrationDummyTime) \right) d\IntegrationDummyTime + \OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant+\FiniteChange \TimeInstant)) \right\} \] Expanding the integral to first order in \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) and applying a first-order Taylor expansion to the terminal cost gives: \[ \OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant+\FiniteChange \TimeInstant)) = \OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)) + \nabla \OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant))^\top \PlantStateRate(\TimeInstant) \FiniteChange \TimeInstant + \mathcal{O}(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant^2) \] Substituting the plant dynamics \(\PlantStateRate = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix\MechanicalPlantState + \StateSpaceInputMatrix\ControlInput\), subtracting \(\OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant))\) from both sides, dividing by \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\), and taking the limit \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant \to 0\) yields the continuous-time Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equation: \[ 0 = \min_{\ControlInput} \left[ \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrStateWeightMatrix \MechanicalPlantState + \ControlInput^\top \LqrControlWeightMatrix \ControlInput + \nabla \OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top (\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\MechanicalPlantState + \StateSpaceInputMatrix\ControlInput) \right] \] Postulating a quadratic value function ansatz (the optimal continuous critic) \(\OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState) = \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState\) with symmetric matrix \(\LqrCostToGoMatrix=\LqrCostToGoMatrix^\top\) (positive definite under the controllability and observability assumptions stated below), its spatial gradient evaluates to: \[ \nabla \OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState) = 2 \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState \] Differentiating the expression inside the bracket with respect to control input \(\ControlInput\) and setting the derivative to zero yields the stationarity condition: \[ 2 \LqrControlWeightMatrix \ControlInput + 2 \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState = \ZeroMatrix \implies \ControlInput^*(\TimeInstant) = -\LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) = -\LqrFeedbackGain \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) \] The optimal state-feedback control law (the optimal actor policy) is strictly linear in the state, with constant optimal gain matrix \(\LqrFeedbackGain = \LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix\). Substituting this optimal control law \(\ControlInput^*\) back into the HJB equation eliminates the minimization: \[ \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrStateWeightMatrix \MechanicalPlantState + \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceInputMatrix \LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState + 2 \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \MechanicalPlantState - 2 \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceInputMatrix \LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState = 0 \] Combining quadratic terms and using the matrix transpose identity \(2 \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \MechanicalPlantState = \MechanicalPlantState^\top (\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceSystemMatrix) \MechanicalPlantState\) reveals: \[ \MechanicalPlantState^\top \left( \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceInputMatrix \LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrStateWeightMatrix \right) \MechanicalPlantState = 0 \quad \forall \MechanicalPlantState \in \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension \] Because this equality must hold identically for all states \(\MechanicalPlantState\), the bracketed matrix equation must vanish, yielding the canonical Continuous Algebraic Riccati Equation (ARE): \[ \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \LqrCostToGoMatrix \StateSpaceInputMatrix \LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrStateWeightMatrix = \ZeroMatrix \] Substituting the control law into the plant dynamics yields the closed-loop system \(\PlantStateRate = (\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrFeedbackGain)\MechanicalPlantState\). When \((\StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \StateSpaceInputMatrix)\) is controllable and \((\StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \LqrStateWeightMatrix^{1/2})\) is observable, all closed-loop eigenvalues are strictly Hurwitz (\(\operatorname{Re}(\MatrixEigenvalue_i(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrFeedbackGain)) < 0\)), guaranteeing exponential convergence to the origin.

Bellman, Richard. 1957. Dynamic Programming. Princeton University Press.

LQR as an optimal closed-loop error dissipator

Beyond minimizing quadratic cost, the Riccati solution \(\LqrCostToGoMatrix \succ \ZeroMatrix\) provides a Lyapunov function for the nominal closed loop. With a positive-definite running cost it also supplies a strict decrease certificate. Differentiating \(\OptimalCostToGoFunction^*(\MechanicalPlantState) = \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState\) along the closed-loop trajectories of \(\PlantStateRate = (\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrFeedbackGain)\MechanicalPlantState\) gives: \[ \begin{aligned} \OptimalCostToGoDerivative^*(\MechanicalPlantState) &= \PlantStateRate^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \MechanicalPlantState + \MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \PlantStateRate = \MechanicalPlantState^\top \left[ (\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrFeedbackGain)^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrCostToGoMatrix (\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrFeedbackGain) \right] \MechanicalPlantState \\ &= \MechanicalPlantState^\top \left[ \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrCostToGoMatrix\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - 2\LqrCostToGoMatrix\StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1}\StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top\LqrCostToGoMatrix \right] \MechanicalPlantState \end{aligned} \] Substituting the Algebraic Riccati Equation \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix + \LqrCostToGoMatrix\StateSpaceSystemMatrix = \LqrCostToGoMatrix\StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1}\StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top\LqrCostToGoMatrix - \LqrStateWeightMatrix\) into this expression yields: \[ \OptimalCostToGoDerivative^*(\MechanicalPlantState) = \MechanicalPlantState^\top \left[ -\LqrStateWeightMatrix - \LqrCostToGoMatrix\StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1}\StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top\LqrCostToGoMatrix \right] \MechanicalPlantState = -\MechanicalPlantState^\top \left[ \LqrStateWeightMatrix + \LqrFeedbackGain^\top \LqrControlWeightMatrix \LqrFeedbackGain \right] \MechanicalPlantState \le 0 \] The derivative is strictly negative when the running-cost matrix is positive definite; with semidefinite \(\LqrStateWeightMatrix\) it can vanish at a nonzero state, even though observability establishes asymptotic convergence. The equality identifies the dissipated quantity as cost, not necessarily physical energy. The value function decreases at a rate matching the instantaneous running cost \(\MechanicalPlantState^\top \LqrStateWeightMatrix \MechanicalPlantState + \ControlInput^\top \LqrControlWeightMatrix \ControlInput\).

Robustness margins of continuous LQR

Continuous-time full-state LQR has a useful robustness property (Åström and Murray 2021) under the ideal full-state assumptions, expressed by the frequency-domain Kalman return difference equality: \[ \left( \IdentityMatrix + \OpenLoopTransferMatrix(\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency) \right)^* \LqrControlWeightMatrix \left( \IdentityMatrix + \OpenLoopTransferMatrix(\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency) \right) = \LqrControlWeightMatrix + \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top (-\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency \IdentityMatrix - \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top)^{-1} \LqrStateWeightMatrix (\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency \IdentityMatrix - \StateSpaceSystemMatrix)^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix \] where \(^*\) denotes conjugate transpose and \(\OpenLoopTransferMatrix(\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency) = \LqrFeedbackGain(\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency\IdentityMatrix - \StateSpaceSystemMatrix)^{-1}\StateSpaceInputMatrix\) is the open-loop frequency response broken at the plant input. Because \(\LqrStateWeightMatrix \succeq \ZeroMatrix\), the second term on the right-hand side is positive semi-definite at frequencies where the resolvents exist. For a single-input system with scalar \(\LqrControlWeightMatrix > 0\), this establishes: \[ |1 + \OpenLoopTransferFunction(\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency)| \ge 1 \quad \forall \AngularFrequency \in \mathbb{R} \] Geometrically on the Nyquist plane, the loop transfer locus \(\OpenLoopTransferFunction(\ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency)\) never enters the open unit disk centered at \(-1 + \ImaginaryUnit0\). This property confers two guaranteed stability margins:

  1. Infinite Gain Margin: The closed loop remains asymptotically stable under scalar gain scaling \(\LoopGainScalingFactor \in (1/2, \infty)\) inserted into the control loop.
  2. Phase Margin Guarantee: The distance from \(-1\) to the Nyquist curve is at least \(1\), which by elementary trigonometry guarantees at least \(60^\circ\) of phase margin for the single-input loop: \[ \PhaseMargin \ge 2 \arcsin(1/2) = 60^\circ \]

These are continuous-time, full-state, nominal-model margins at the specified loop break. They are not an unrestricted guarantee against actuator dynamics, structural resonances, or simultaneous multivariable perturbations. Digital sampling and transport add phase lag; section 1.5.5 gives the low-frequency delay approximation used to budget that loss.

Discrete-time LQR

For digital microcontrollers executing at sampling period \(\SamplingPeriod\), the discrete-time LQR formulation minimizes: \[ \DiscreteLqrQuadraticObjective = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \left( \MechanicalPlantState_k^\top \LqrStateWeightMatrix \MechanicalPlantState_k + \ControlInput_k^\top \LqrControlWeightMatrix \ControlInput_k \right) \] subject to \(\MechanicalPlantState_{k+1} = \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix \MechanicalPlantState_k + \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix \ControlInput_k\). The matrix \(\LqrCostToGoMatrix\) satisfies the Discrete Algebraic Riccati Equation (DARE): \[ \LqrCostToGoMatrix = \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix - \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix \left( \LqrControlWeightMatrix + \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix \right)^{-1} \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix + \LqrStateWeightMatrix \] yielding the discrete optimal state-feedback gain: \[ \DiscreteLqrFeedbackGain = \left( \LqrControlWeightMatrix + \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix \right)^{-1} \DiscreteStateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix \DiscreteStateSpaceSystemMatrix, \quad \ControlInput_k^* = -\DiscreteLqrFeedbackGain \MechanicalPlantState_k \]

The separation principle and linear quadratic Gaussian control

In physical deployments, full-state access is rarely available; controllers must estimate \(\StateEstimate\) from noisy sensor observations \(\MeasurementVector = \MeasurementMatrix\MechanicalPlantState + \MeasurementNoiseVector\). Under a linear model with independent Gaussian white process and measurement noises of intensities \(\ProcessNoiseIntensity\) and \(\ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity\), the optimal continuous state estimator is the continuous-time Kalman-Bucy filter (section 1.8; the discrete filter the machine runs is in Estimating State from Dated Measurements): \[ \StateEstimateDerivative(\TimeInstant) = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix\StateEstimate(\TimeInstant) + \StateSpaceInputMatrix\ControlInput(\TimeInstant) + \KalmanBucyObserverGain\left(\MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant) - \MeasurementMatrix\StateEstimate(\TimeInstant)\right) \] where \(\MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant) - \MeasurementMatrix\StateEstimate(\TimeInstant)\) is the innovation (the measurement residual), and the optimal Kalman observer gain is: \[ \KalmanBucyObserverGain = \SteadyStateErrorCovariance\MeasurementMatrix^\top \ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity^{-1} \] At steady state, \(\SteadyStateErrorCovariance = \mathbb{E}[(\MechanicalPlantState - \StateEstimate)(\MechanicalPlantState - \StateEstimate)^\top]\) is the state estimation error covariance matrix, which solves the dual filter algebraic Riccati equation under the usual stabilizability and detectability conditions: \[ \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \SteadyStateErrorCovariance + \SteadyStateErrorCovariance \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top - \SteadyStateErrorCovariance \MeasurementMatrix^\top \ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity^{-1} \MeasurementMatrix \SteadyStateErrorCovariance + \ProcessNoiseIntensity = \ZeroMatrix \]

To an ML practitioner, the Kalman filter is the exact recursive Bayesian Minimum Mean Square Error (MMSE) estimator. It continuously updates the posterior state distribution \(\GaussianDistribution(\StateEstimate, \SteadyStateErrorCovariance)\) by balancing dynamic model propagation (growing uncertainty via process noise \(\ProcessNoiseIntensity\)) against empirical sensor evidence (contracting uncertainty via inverse continuous measurement-noise intensity \(\ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity^{-1}\)). For discrete measurements, the analogous update uses the per-sample measurement covariance \(\MeasurementNoiseCovariance\), not the continuous noise intensity. With invertible prior and measurement covariances, it evaluates as: \[ \UpdatedErrorCovariance^{-1} = \PredictedErrorCovariance^{-1} + \MeasurementMatrix^\top \MeasurementNoiseCovariance^{-1} \MeasurementMatrix, \quad \UpdatedErrorCovariance^{-1} \UpdatedStateEstimate = \PredictedErrorCovariance^{-1} \PredictedStateEstimate + \MeasurementMatrix^\top \MeasurementNoiseCovariance^{-1} \MeasurementVector_k \] In information (precision) space, the posterior precision is the linear addition of prior dynamic precision \(\PredictedErrorCovariance^{-1}\) and measurement evidence precision \(\MeasurementMatrix^\top \MeasurementNoiseCovariance^{-1} \MeasurementMatrix\). This reveals the Kalman filter as the exact recursive, online counterpart of Bayesian linear regression and Gaussian process conditioning, and highlights the mathematical duality between optimal control (LQR) and optimal estimation (Kalman filter):

Duality Attribute Optimal Regulator (LQR) Optimal Estimator (Kalman Filter)
System Operator Drift matrix \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix\) Adjoint drift matrix \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top\)
Control / Sensor Map Actuator input matrix \(\StateSpaceInputMatrix\) Sensor output matrix transpose \(\MeasurementMatrix^\top\)
Weighting / Noise Covariance State weight \(\LqrStateWeightMatrix \succeq \ZeroMatrix\), control effort \(\LqrControlWeightMatrix \succ \ZeroMatrix\) Process noise \(\ProcessNoiseIntensity \succeq \ZeroMatrix\), measurement noise \(\ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity \succ \ZeroMatrix\)
Riccati Solution Control Riccati matrix \(\LqrCostToGoMatrix\) Estimation covariance matrix \(\SteadyStateErrorCovariance\)
Optimal Gain Feedback gain \(\LqrFeedbackGain = \LqrControlWeightMatrix^{-1} \StateSpaceInputMatrix^\top \LqrCostToGoMatrix\) Observer gain \(\KalmanBucyObserverGain = \SteadyStateErrorCovariance \MeasurementMatrix^\top \ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity^{-1}\)
Optimality Criterion Minimizes quadratic state and action penalty Minimizes trace of estimation error covariance \(\mathbb{E}[\lVert\MechanicalPlantState-\StateEstimate\rVert^2]\)

The Linear Quadratic Gaussian (LQG) controller cascades this optimal estimator with the optimal LQR regulator by applying feedback directly to the estimated state: \[ \ControlInput(\TimeInstant) = -\LqrFeedbackGain \StateEstimate(\TimeInstant) \] The Separation Principle (Åström and Murray 2021) proves that under linear-Gaussian assumptions, observer design and state-feedback controller design are completely decoupled. Specifically, the poles of the closed-loop LQG system are strictly the union of the controller poles \(\operatorname{eig}(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \StateSpaceInputMatrix\LqrFeedbackGain)\) and the observer poles \(\operatorname{eig}(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix - \KalmanBucyObserverGain\MeasurementMatrix)\).

Systems Perspective 1.1: The Doyle warning: LQG robustness margins
While full-state LQR provides guaranteed \(60^\circ\) phase margins and infinite gain margins, John Doyle’s classic 1978 paper demonstrated that output-feedback LQG controllers possess no guaranteed stability margins whatsoever (Åström and Murray 2021). When an observer is inserted into the loop, unmodeled plant parameter variations couple estimation error \(\StateEstimationError = \MechanicalPlantState - \StateEstimate\) directly back into the control actuation channel. Even infinitesimal parameter perturbations can then push the closed-loop poles across the imaginary axis into the unstable right-half plane.

For physical AI systems, this carries a warning. Cascading a high-capacity neural perception system (e.g., an end-to-end vision model estimating 6D object poses or end-effector coordinates) with an optimal tracking controller does not inherit the controller’s nominal robustness. Dynamic perception lag, out-of-distribution observation errors, and high-frequency sensor noise degrade feedback phase margin rapidly. An implementation must evaluate the combined estimator-controller dynamics and its safety bounds; loop transfer recovery is one design technique, not a universal requirement (The Per-Tick Gatekeeper).

Stability Theory and Model Predictive Control

An arm controller that converges exponentially to a target grasp pose can still swing its elbow through a cage-door post along the way. Stability concerns whether trajectories remain near, or converge to, an equilibrium; set invariance concerns whether they stay inside a safe region at every instant. These are separate claims, each conditional on a model, an initial-state domain, and the implementation assumptions.

Lyapunov stability and invariant sets

Continuous gradient flow provides a useful analogy for Lyapunov analysis. It evolves parameter weights according to \(\ParameterVelocity = -\nabla_\ModelParameters \LossFunction(\ModelParameters)\). Along this parameter trajectory, the empirical loss decreases monotonically via the multivariable chain rule: \[ \LossTimeDerivative(\ModelParameters) = \nabla_\ModelParameters\LossFunction(\ModelParameters)^\top \ParameterVelocity = -\nabla_\ModelParameters\LossFunction(\ModelParameters)^\top \nabla_\ModelParameters\LossFunction(\ModelParameters) = -\|\nabla_\ModelParameters\LossFunction(\ModelParameters)\|^2 \le 0 \] In parameter optimization, the software program has unconstrained, direct write access to the parameter velocity vector \(\ParameterVelocity\), allowing the optimizer to force loss descent along the negative gradient direction. In physical control, the robot’s physical state \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) \in \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension\) evolves in physical state space according to continuous differential equations \(\PlantStateRate = \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\). Unlike software network weights \(\ModelParameters\), physical coordinates \(\MechanicalPlantState\) cannot be updated by arbitrary gradient descent: actuators cannot command arbitrary velocity vectors \(\PlantStateRate\), because physical momentum, mass inertia, unforced drift, and kinematic constraints govern the system. By Newton’s second law, motor actuators modulate second-order accelerations (\(\GeneralizedAccelerations = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^{-1}(\JointTorque - \NonlinearBiasTorque)\)), which must be integrated through continuous time against centrifugal forces, Coriolis couplings, and gravitational fields.

Instead, a Lyapunov function \(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) acts as an analytical, continuous state-space loss function that certifies the asymptotic convergence of the physical plant’s continuous trajectories without simulating every rollout to infinity. In mechanical systems, \(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) often corresponds to the physical or synthetic Hamiltonian energy of the plant: kinetic energy stored in moving mass plus potential energy stored in gravitational elevation or virtual elastic springs.

Consider an autonomous nonlinear dynamical system: \[ \PlantStateRate(\TimeInstant) = \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)), \quad \ControlAffineDriftField(\ZeroMatrix) = \ZeroMatrix \] where \(\MechanicalPlantState^* = \ZeroMatrix\) is an isolated equilibrium point, and \(\ControlAffineDriftField: \LyapunovAnalysisDomain \to \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension\) is locally Lipschitz on an open domain \(\LyapunovAnalysisDomain \subset \mathbb{R}^\StateDimension\) containing the origin.

A continuously differentiable scalar function \(\LyapunovFunction: \LyapunovAnalysisDomain \to \mathbb{R}\) is a candidate Lyapunov function if it is positive definite on \(\LyapunovAnalysisDomain\): \[ \LyapunovFunction(\ZeroMatrix) = 0, \quad \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) > 0 \quad \forall \MechanicalPlantState \in \LyapunovAnalysisDomain \setminus \{\ZeroMatrix\} \] The directional derivative of \(\LyapunovFunction\) along the trajectories of \(\PlantStateRate = \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\) evaluates via the multivariable chain rule: \[ \LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \PlantStateRate = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \cdot \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) \] Control theory traditionally denotes this inner product as the Lie derivative \(\DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \cdot \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\). In computational terms, this is the directional derivative or forward-mode Jacobian-vector product (JVP) of the scalar loss \(\LyapunovFunction\) evaluated in the direction of the physical drift vector field \(\ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\): \[ \operatorname{JVP}(\LyapunovFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)) = \left. \frac{\DerivativeOperator}{\DerivativeOperator\DirectionalDerivativeStep} \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState + \DirectionalDerivativeStep \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)) \right|_{\DirectionalDerivativeStep = 0} = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) = \DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \] Automatic differentiation can evaluate this directional derivative without forming a full Jacobian. Computing it at sampled states, however, does not verify its sign everywhere in a region.

Alexander Lyapunov established three foundational stability tiers (Slotine and Li 1991):

  1. Stability in the Sense of Lyapunov: If \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState) \le 0\) for all \(\MechanicalPlantState \in \LyapunovAnalysisDomain\), the origin is stable. Trajectories starting arbitrarily close to the origin remain trapped within bounded neighborhood contours for all \(\TimeInstant \ge 0\).
  2. Asymptotic Stability: If \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState) < 0\) for all \(\MechanicalPlantState \in \LyapunovAnalysisDomain \setminus \{\ZeroMatrix\}\), the origin is asymptotically stable: \(\lim_{t \to \infty} \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) = \ZeroMatrix\). The system is guaranteed to descend the energy landscape toward the origin.
  3. Exponential Stability: If there exist positive constants \(\ExponentialStabilityConstant_1, \ExponentialStabilityConstant_2, \ExponentialStabilityConstant_3 > 0\) such that \(\ExponentialStabilityConstant_1 \|\MechanicalPlantState\|^2 \le \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \le \ExponentialStabilityConstant_2 \|\MechanicalPlantState\|^2\) and \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState) \le -\ExponentialStabilityConstant_3 \|\MechanicalPlantState\|^2\), trajectories converge exponentially: \[ \|\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)\| \le \sqrt{\frac{\ExponentialStabilityConstant_2}{\ExponentialStabilityConstant_1}} \|\MechanicalPlantState_0\| e^{-\frac{\ExponentialStabilityConstant_3}{2 \ExponentialStabilityConstant_2} \TimeInstant} \quad \forall \TimeInstant \ge 0 \]

For mechanical systems with friction and viscous damping, \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState)\) often vanishes on a subspace (for example, when velocities are zero while position errors remain nonzero: \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) = -\GeneralizedVelocities^\top \ViscousDampingMatrix \GeneralizedVelocities = 0\) whenever \(\GeneralizedVelocities = \ZeroMatrix\)). Standard Lyapunov theorems only certify stability in the sense of Lyapunov, failing to prove asymptotic convergence. LaSalle’s Invariance Principle (Slotine and Li 1991) resolves this. If \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState) \le 0\) on a compact, positively invariant set \(\LaSalleInvariantSet\), all trajectories starting in \(\LaSalleInvariantSet\) asymptotically converge to the largest invariant set \(\LargestInvariantSet\) contained within \(\{ \MechanicalPlantState \in \LaSalleInvariantSet \mid \LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState) = 0 \}\). Because any trajectory trapped on \(\LargestInvariantSet\) with \(\GeneralizedVelocities \equiv \ZeroMatrix\) must also satisfy zero acceleration (\(\GeneralizedAccelerations \equiv \ZeroMatrix\)), the equations of motion force position errors to vanish (\(\PidProportionalGainMatrix \JointTrackingError = \ZeroMatrix \implies \JointTrackingError = \ZeroMatrix\)). LaSalle’s principle proves that the only trajectory that can remain indefinitely in the zero-dissipation manifold is the origin itself, certifying asymptotic convergence.

A compact sublevel set \(\LyapunovSublevelSet = \{ \MechanicalPlantState \in \LyapunovAnalysisDomain \mid \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \le \SublevelSetThreshold \}\) contained in \(\LyapunovAnalysisDomain\) is positively invariant when \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative \le 0\) throughout it. It is an inner estimate of the domain of attraction (DOA) only when trajectories there also converge to the equilibrium, for example by strict descent or the LaSalle argument. Invariance alone does not imply convergence.

A neural network can parameterize a candidate Lyapunov function and search for a stabilizing policy. For example, \(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = \MechanicalPlantState^\top \NeuralLyapunovFactor(\MechanicalPlantState) \NeuralLyapunovFactor(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \MechanicalPlantState + \LyapunovPositivityMargin\lVert\MechanicalPlantState\rVert^2\), with \(\LyapunovPositivityMargin>0\), guarantees positivity away from the origin. Convexity alone does not guarantee this property. Penalizing violations of \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative<0\) during training produces a candidate; a certificate requires verifying the derivative condition throughout the claimed region, including the model and implementation uncertainty.

Control Lyapunov functions and stabilization

For a controlled system \(\PlantStateRate = \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) + \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)\ControlInput\), the directional derivative along the closed-loop dynamics splits linearly into unforced drift and control effectiveness: \[ \LyapunovFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState, \ControlInput) = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \cdot (\ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) + \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)\ControlInput) = \DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) + \InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ControlInput \] where \(\DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \cdot \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) \in \mathbb{R}\) is the unforced directional drift and \(\InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState) \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times \ControlInputDimension}\) is the control effectiveness gradient. A candidate Lyapunov function \(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) is a Control Lyapunov Function (CLF) (Ames et al. 2014; Slotine and Li 1991) if there exists an admissible control input \(\ControlInput \in \AdmissibleControlSet\) that forces \(\LyapunovFunction\) to decrease at every non-equilibrium state: \[ \inf_{\ControlInput \in \AdmissibleControlSet} \left[ \DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) + \InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ControlInput + \ClfClassKFunction(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)) \right] \le 0 \quad \forall \MechanicalPlantState \in \LyapunovAnalysisDomain \setminus \{\ZeroMatrix\} \] where \(\ClfClassKFunction\) is an extended class \(\ClassKFunctionSet\) function (a strictly increasing function with \(\ClfClassKFunction(0)=0\)). For ML practitioners, \(\ClfClassKFunction(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState))\) acts as a state-dependent convergence rate floor: the larger the current error \(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\), the steeper the required rate of descent. Geometrically, the CLF inequality defines a closed affine half-space in control space \(\ClfDescentControlSet(\MechanicalPlantState) = \{ \ControlInput \in \AdmissibleControlSet \mid \InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ControlInput \le -\DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) - \ClfClassKFunction(\LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)) \}\); any control input chosen inside this half-space guarantees monotonic descent of the continuous state-space loss.

Ames, Aaron D, Jessy W Grizzle, and Paulo Tabuada. 2014. “Control Barrier Function Based Quadratic Programs with Application to Adaptive Cruise Control.” 53rd IEEE Conference on Decision and Control, 6271–78.

Sontag’s universal stabilizing formula

For an unconstrained control-affine system, a smooth CLF admits the following stabilizing feedback construction (Slotine and Li 1991): \[ \SontagStabilizingControl(\MechanicalPlantState) = \begin{cases} -\left( \frac{\DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) + \sqrt{(\DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState))^2 + \|\InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\|^4}}{\|\InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\|^2} \right) \left( \InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \right)^\top & \text{if } \InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \neq \ZeroMatrix \\[8pt] \ZeroMatrix & \text{if } \InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = \ZeroMatrix \end{cases} \] The formula makes \(\LyapunovFunctionDerivative<0\) away from the origin when the CLF condition holds. Continuity at the origin additionally requires the small control property: arbitrarily small states can be stabilized with arbitrarily small inputs. The condition \(\DriftLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction<0\) wherever \(\InputLieDerivativeOperator \LyapunovFunction=0\) is the CLF’s uncontrollable-direction condition, not the small control property itself.
