A heartbeat timeout is an alternative trigger with its own response path, not an extra countdown appended to an expired chunk lease. If clearance is instead referenced to a hazard’s appearance, the bound must also include any time until the supporting observation captures it.

Under constant cruising speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) before brake onset, a verified lower bound on mechanical braking deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration > 0\), a localization bound \(\LocalizationBound\), and a fixed protective clearance \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\), the total stopping distance bounded by available clearance \(\ClearDistance\) satisfies: \[ \SpeedAtDetection\,(\LeaseExpiryDelaySubtotal + \DispatchAdmissionInterval) + \frac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration} + \LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin \le \ClearDistance \] Solving directly for the maximum admissible validity lease \(\ChunkLease\) yields: \[ \ChunkLease \le \frac{\ClearDistance - \LocalizationBound - \ProtectiveClearanceMargin - \frac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration}}{\SpeedAtDetection} - \left( \DetectionInterval + \FallbackHold + \DispatchObservationAge + \DispatchAdmissionInterval + \ActuatorResponseTime \right) \] This constant-deceleration model is a reference calculation, not the complete stopping budget of the running machine. A shaped stop must substitute its qualified stopping distance for \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration)\) and reserve tracking error and every other justified allowance, and the same substitution applies to the teleoperation bound below. If the right-hand side is nonpositive, this budget permits no positive execution lease. The enforcer must select the available fallback immediately, because a negative bound means that even an immediate response lacks the stated stopping reservation.

Teleoperation network round-trip time bounds

When a human supervisor remotely teleoperates the robot across an external wireless link, the control loop includes human perceptual reaction latency and network packet transport delays, decomposed into five terms:

  • \(\TeleoperationVideoLatency\) represent video camera exposure, encoding, and operator display processing, excluding network transport, which is charged in \(\TeleoperationRoundTripTime\).
  • \(\HumanReactionTime\) denote human neuromuscular reaction time (\(\HumanReactionTime \approx 150\text{--}250\text{ ms}\)).
  • \(\TeleoperationRoundTripTime = \TeleoperationDownlinkTime + \TeleoperationUplinkTime\) represent network round-trip transport latency.
  • \(\ActuatorResponseTime\) denote actuator braking engagement lag.
  • \(\BrakingDistance = \frac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration}\) represent the purely mechanical braking distance.

Total available time before available clearance \(\ClearDistance\) is exhausted is bounded by \(\ClearanceTimeBudget = (\ClearDistance - \BrakingDistance) / \SpeedAtDetection\). The allowable round-trip network latency ceiling is: \[ \TeleoperationRoundTripTime \le \frac{\ClearDistance - \BrakingDistance}{\SpeedAtDetection} - \left( \TeleoperationVideoLatency + \HumanReactionTime + \ActuatorResponseTime \right) \] If network jitter pushes \(\TeleoperationRoundTripTime\) beyond this bound, the human operator cannot perceive an obstacle and deliver a corrective command before the vehicle exhausts its stopping distance. Consequently, the local enforcer must evaluate physical invariants at a rate justified by its response budget, revoking operator command authority and executing autonomous braking before physical clearance is lost. Human Authority, Reaction Time, and Clearance uses this bound to size a handover, where the operator’s round trip spends the same pre-brake budget.

Kinodynamic bounds vs. wave-variable passivity

Transport delay in teleoperation creates two distinct failure modes: spatial boundary breach (kinematic collision) and high-frequency dynamic chatter (loss of closed-loop passivity). Wave variables (section 1.10) preserve the communication channel’s passivity under their delay assumptions. The coupled teleoperator still requires a valid energy argument for its other components. Passivity does not bound physical displacement. Safety requires satisfying both invariants simultaneously. Wave variables bound interactive contact energy, while the kinodynamic latency inequality reserves spatial clearance under its constant-speed, fixed-obstacle, and braking assumptions.

Rigid Body Contact Mechanics

In free space, a small change in joint torque produces a smooth change in link acceleration. When a gripper finger or foot touches a rigid surface, the gap closes to zero and a normal reaction force appears abruptly to prevent interpenetration. Simulating and controlling physical interaction (locomotion, dexterous manipulation, tool assembly) requires modeling this boundary transition. The ideal unilateral, nonadhesive rigid-contact model uses complementarity: interaction forces arise strictly when bodies establish physical contact.

Let \(\GeneralizedCoordinates \in \mathbb{R}^\GeneralizedCoordinateCount\) denote generalized configuration coordinates. Let \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) be the signed distance (or gap function) between contact geometries:

  • \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) > 0\): Bodies are separated.
  • \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = 0\): Bodies are touching (contact established).
  • \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) < 0\): Bodies are penetrating (a geometric interpenetration that rigid mechanics strictly forbids).

Let \(\ComplementarityNormalForce\) (or \(\Glyph{f}_n\)) denote the normal contact force pushing the bodies apart. In constrained mathematical optimization, the non-penetration condition corresponds to the inequality constraint \(\InequalityConstraintFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ge 0\). The governing physical law of unilateral rigid contact is precisely the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) complementary slackness condition:

  1. Non-penetration (primal feasibility): \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ge 0\)
  2. Repulsive unilateral force (dual feasibility): \(\ComplementarityNormalForce \ge 0\)
  3. Complementary slackness: \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ComplementarityNormalForce = 0\)

These non-smooth physical contact laws map onto familiar mathematical concepts of constrained optimization:

Physical Entity Mechanical Role in Physical Plant Optimization Duality in ML Systems
Gap Function \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ge 0\) Spatial clearance preventing interpenetration Primal inequality constraint \(\InequalityConstraintFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ge 0\) defining the feasible region
Normal Force \(\ComplementarityNormalForce \ge 0\) Repulsive contact force pushing bodies apart Dual Lagrange multiplier \(\ConstraintLagrangeMultiplier \ge 0\) associated with the active constraint
Complementary Slackness \(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ComplementarityNormalForce = 0\) Contact force exists only when touching (\(\ContactGapFunction = 0\)) KKT condition: multiplier is nonzero only when constraint is active
Delassus Operator \(\DelassusOperator\) Apparent inverse inertia reflected at contact point Schur complement of the constraint Jacobian in the KKT system
Impact Impulse \(\NormalContactImpulse = \EffectiveImpactMass \FiniteChange \ContactNormalVelocity\) Instantaneous momentum transfer across zero duration Non-differentiable jump in state trajectory; Dirac delta distribution
Restitution Law \(\PostImpactNormalVelocity = -\RestitutionCoefficient \PreImpactNormalVelocity\) Discontinuous velocity jump across collision boundary Step discontinuity that breaks smooth reverse-mode automatic differentiation

Just as Lagrange multipliers in constrained optimization become nonzero (\(\ConstraintLagrangeMultiplier > 0\)) only when constraints are active and tight (\(\InequalityConstraintFunction = 0\)), normal contact forces materialize exclusively upon contact closure:

  • If bodies are separated (\(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) > 0\)), the constraint is inactive, enforcing \(\ComplementarityNormalForce = 0\).
  • If bodies are touching (\(\ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = 0\)), the constraint is active, permitting repulsive normal forces \(\ComplementarityNormalForce \ge 0\) to prevent interpenetration.
  • This nonadhesive contact model excludes tensile normal forces (\(\ComplementarityNormalForce<0\)); adhesion, grasping, and attached fixtures need other models.

The Delassus operator and acceleration-level LCP

At the velocity level, kinematic normal velocity is \(\ContactNormalVelocity = \ContactGapVelocity(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedVelocities\), where \(\ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \nabla \ContactGapFunction(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times \GeneralizedCoordinateCount}\) is the contact normal Jacobian. Differentiating with respect to continuous time yields normal acceleration: \[ \ContactGapAcceleration(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \ContactNormalJacobianDerivative(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities \] Substituting the multi-body equations of motion \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \JointTorque + \ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \ComplementarityNormalForce\) isolates configuration acceleration: \[ \GeneralizedAccelerations = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^{-1} \left( \JointTorque - \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities - \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) + \ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \ComplementarityNormalForce \right) \] Substituting \(\GeneralizedAccelerations\) back into the normal acceleration expression yields: \[ \ContactGapAcceleration = \DelassusOperator(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ComplementarityNormalForce + \ContactDriftAcceleration \] where the scalar \(\DelassusOperator(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^{-1} \ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \ge 0\) is the Delassus operational space compliance operator (the apparent inverse inertia reflected at the contact point), and \(\ContactDriftAcceleration = \ContactNormalJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^{-1}(\JointTorque - \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities - \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)) + \ContactNormalJacobianDerivative(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities\) collects unforced kinematic and dynamic drift accelerations.

When contact is maintained (\(\ContactGapFunction = 0, \ContactGapVelocity = 0\)), non-penetration requires \(\ContactGapAcceleration \ge 0\). The contact mechanics form a canonical Linear Complementarity Problem (LCP): \[ \ContactGapAcceleration \ge 0, \quad \ComplementarityNormalForce \ge 0, \quad \ContactGapAcceleration \cdot \ComplementarityNormalForce = 0 \] Under polyhedral friction cone approximations, this generalizes to a vector LCP solvable via Lemke’s pivoting algorithm or quadratic programming. This is a rigid-contact idealization. Real interfaces deform, and the numerical contact solution must still be checked against the motion and force quantities the application needs.

Non-smooth collision manifolds and velocity jumps

When rigid bodies collide, the impact occurs over an infinitesimal duration \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant \to 0\). For frictionless normal impact against a stationary rigid plane, let \(\PreImpactNormalVelocity < 0\) be the pre-impact normal velocity and \(\RestitutionCoefficient \in [0, 1]\) the coefficient of restitution. The Newton restitution model imposes the velocity jump: \[ \PostImpactNormalVelocity = -\RestitutionCoefficient \PreImpactNormalVelocity \implies \FiniteChange \ContactNormalVelocity = \PostImpactNormalVelocity - \PreImpactNormalVelocity = -(1 + \RestitutionCoefficient) \PreImpactNormalVelocity \] With effective normal mass \(\EffectiveImpactMass\), the normal impulse is \(\NormalContactImpulse = \EffectiveImpactMass \FiniteChange \ContactNormalVelocity\). In an idealized rigid impact, velocity changes discontinuously, meaning the acceleration is a Dirac delta distribution \(\UnitImpulseFunction(\TimeInstant - \RigidImpactInstant)\) rather than an ordinary finite derivative:5 \[ \lim_{\VanishingTimeOffset \to 0^+} \frac{\lVert \GeneralizedVelocities(\RigidImpactInstant+\VanishingTimeOffset) - \GeneralizedVelocities(\RigidImpactInstant-\VanishingTimeOffset)\rVert}{2\VanishingTimeOffset} = \infty \]

5 Moreau Time-Stepping and Non-Smooth Mechanics: Measure-differential models represent ideal impulses without resolving microscopic contact duration. Numerical contact solvers still require a validated geometry, friction law, and step-size study for the plant; a smooth optimizer that omits impact can miss a relevant force transient.

Failure modes for automatic differentiation and BPTT

For a differentiable discrete simulator \(\MechanicalPlantState_{k+1} = \SimulatorStepFunction_k(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ModelParameters)\), define state sensitivity \(\StateParameterSensitivity_k = \partial\MechanicalPlantState_k/\partial\ModelParameters\). The chain rule propagates it as: \[ \StateParameterSensitivity_{k+1} = \frac{\partial \SimulatorStepFunction_k}{\partial\MechanicalPlantState_k}\StateParameterSensitivity_k + \frac{\partial \SimulatorStepFunction_k}{\partial\ModelParameters}. \] For a rollout loss \(\RolloutLoss = \sum_{k=0}^\RolloutStepCount\ell_k(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ModelParameters)\), the parameter gradient is: \[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \RolloutLoss = \sum_{k=0}^\RolloutStepCount\left[\StateParameterSensitivity_k^\top\nabla_{\MechanicalPlantState_k}\ell_k + \nabla_\ModelParameters\ell_k\right]. \] Reverse-mode differentiation evaluates the same chain rule without storing every forward sensitivity. Contact complicates the derivatives of \(\SimulatorStepFunction_k\), not the chain rule itself. Within a fixed contact mode, a transition can have a finite derivative; at a mode change it can be nonsmooth, and the derivative must account for how the impact time changes with the initial state. An acceleration impulse alone does not imply that every state-transition Jacobian diverges.

A solver that differentiates only its currently active branch can miss the sensitivity of contact timing. Likewise, the contact force is zero while bodies are separated, but a distance-based loss can still supply a gradient toward contact. Whether a rollout has flat regions or abrupt changes depends on its dynamics, contact discretization, and objective.

Compliant contact replaces ideal impulses with deformation and finite-duration forces. This can improve differentiability, but the selected force law must remain repulsive and its stiffness can require smaller time steps. Implicit differentiation of converged contact constraints avoids differentiating every solver iteration, provided the active set and regularity conditions support a derivative. Stochastic policy gradients offer another route because they do not require differentiating through the contact transition; their variance and sampling cost remain separate concerns.

Elastodynamic impact force and compliance deformation

When an effective moving mass \(\EffectiveImpactMass\) strikes an obstacle or human tissue, rigid-body approximations break down because real materials deform elastically over a nonzero compression stroke \(\PenetrationDepth(\TimeInstant)\). Let \(\EffectiveSeriesStiffness\) denote the effective contact stiffness across the interface. In series compliance between the manipulator structural chain (\(\RobotStructuralStiffness\)) and human tissue or obstacle (\(\HertzianContactStiffness\)): \[ \frac{1}{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness} = \frac{1}{\RobotStructuralStiffness} + \frac{1}{\HertzianContactStiffness} \]

Unmitigated elastic impact force

For an unmitigated collision at impact velocity \(\SpeedBound\), initial kinetic energy \(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveImpactMass \SpeedBound^2\) converts entirely into elastic strain energy \(\ElasticStrainEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveSeriesStiffness \PeakPenetrationDepth^2\) at the point of maximum compression (\(\PenetrationRate=0\)). Energy conservation yields the peak structural deflection: \[ \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveImpactMass \SpeedBound^2 = \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveSeriesStiffness \PeakPenetrationDepth^2 \implies \PeakPenetrationDepth = \SpeedBound \sqrt{\frac{\EffectiveImpactMass}{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness}} \] The resulting peak transient contact force \(\UnbrakedImpactForce = \EffectiveSeriesStiffness \PeakPenetrationDepth\) evaluates to: \[ \UnbrakedImpactForce = \SpeedBound \sqrt{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness \EffectiveImpactMass} \] This relation applies to a lossless linear spring with fixed effective mass and no additional energy input during compression. It identifies how speed, mass, and stiffness affect the modeled peak; a human-contact limit additionally depends on contact location, area, duration, and the applicable test conditions. The scalar spring calculation alone does not establish compliance with a safety standard.

Coupled contact indentation with mechanical braking

When active mechanical brakes engage with constant opposing force \(\EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) throughout compression, the braking system performs mechanical work \(\BrakingWork = \EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration \PenetrationDepth\) across the indentation depth \(\PenetrationDepth\). The coupled energy balance becomes: \[ \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveImpactMass \ImpactSpeed^2 = \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveSeriesStiffness \PeakPenetrationDepth^2 + \EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration \PeakPenetrationDepth \] Rearranging into quadratic form: \[ \PeakPenetrationDepth^2 + \frac{2 \EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness} \PeakPenetrationDepth - \frac{\EffectiveImpactMass \ImpactSpeed^2}{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness} = 0 \] Solving for the physically admissible positive deformation root: \[ \PeakPenetrationDepth = \sqrt{\left(\frac{\EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness}\right)^2 + \frac{\EffectiveImpactMass \ImpactSpeed^2}{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness}} - \frac{\EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{\EffectiveSeriesStiffness} \] When \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration = 0\), this expression smoothly recovers the unmitigated deformation \(\PeakPenetrationDepth = \ImpactSpeed\sqrt{\EffectiveImpactMass/\EffectiveSeriesStiffness}\). For the unbraked spring model, the quarter-cycle compression duration is \(\QuarterCycleCompressionTime = \frac{\pi}{2}\sqrt{\EffectiveImpactMass/\EffectiveSeriesStiffness}\). This is not the peak time of the actively braked model. Reactive braking triggered only after physical contact cannot attenuate the unbraked initial force peak if sensor detection and brake engagement latency exceed this \(\QuarterCycleCompressionTime\). Consequently, safety cases cannot substitute reactive post-impact braking for verified pre-impact separation clearance envelopes (\(\PreImpactSeparationDistance \ge \StoppingDistance\)).

Wheel slip ratio and friction boundaries

For wheeled mobile robots and terrestrial platforms, tractive force transmission between the wheel contact patch and the ground plane is governed by non-linear slip dynamics. The longitudinal slip ratio \(\LongitudinalSlipRatio\) quantifies the kinematic velocity mismatch between the wheel circumferential speed \(\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed\) (wheel radius \(\WheelRadius\), angular rate \(\WheelAngularSpeed\)) and the vehicle forward longitudinal speed \(\LongitudinalVehicleSpeed\): \[ \LongitudinalSlipRatio = \begin{cases} \frac{\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed - \LongitudinalVehicleSpeed}{\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed}, & \text{under acceleration } (\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed > \LongitudinalVehicleSpeed \ge 0) \\[6pt] \frac{\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed - \LongitudinalVehicleSpeed}{\LongitudinalVehicleSpeed}, & \text{under braking } (\LongitudinalVehicleSpeed > \WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed \ge 0) \end{cases} \] For forward motion with nonnegative wheel speed, the same convention can be regularized near standstill: \[ \LongitudinalSlipRatio = \frac{\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed - \LongitudinalVehicleSpeed}{\max(|\LongitudinalVehicleSpeed|, |\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed|, \SlipRegularizationSpeed)} \in [-1, 1] \] The bound \([-1,1]\) assumes \(\LongitudinalVehicleSpeed\) and \(\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed\) have the same sign; reverse motion needs a declared direction convention. The endpoint values below also assume the nonzero speed exceeds \(\SlipRegularizationSpeed\). Here \(\LongitudinalSlipRatio = 0\) corresponds to pure rolling without slip, \(\LongitudinalSlipRatio = 1\) denotes complete drive-wheel spin-out (\(\LongitudinalVehicleSpeed = 0, \WheelAngularSpeed > 0\)), and \(\LongitudinalSlipRatio = -1\) corresponds to complete wheel lock-up (\(\WheelRadius\WheelAngularSpeed = 0, \LongitudinalVehicleSpeed > 0\)).

Tractive force \(\TractiveForce\) scales with the normal contact load \(\WheelNormalLoad\) through the longitudinal friction coefficient \(\LongitudinalFrictionCoefficient(\LongitudinalSlipRatio)\): \[ \TractiveForce(\LongitudinalSlipRatio) = \LongitudinalFrictionCoefficient(\LongitudinalSlipRatio) \WheelNormalLoad \] At low slip ratios (\(|\LongitudinalSlipRatio| < \PeakSlipRatio\)), where the peak depends on tire and surface conditions, tire or tread rubber shears elastically. Tractive force is approximately linear in slip: \(\TractiveForce \approx \LongitudinalTractionStiffness \LongitudinalSlipRatio\), with longitudinal traction stiffness \(\LongitudinalTractionStiffness\). Beyond \(\PeakSlipRatio\), adhesion collapses into gross sliding friction. The slope \(\DerivativeOperator\LongitudinalFrictionCoefficient / \DerivativeOperator\LongitudinalSlipRatio\) turns negative, which can destabilize a torque-controlled wheel-speed equilibrium. If an ungrounded policy commands drive motor torque exceeding peak traction (\(\CommandedTractiveForce > \PeakTractionCoefficient \WheelNormalLoad\)), the wheel rapidly accelerates into the unstable slip regime (\(\DerivativeOperator\WheelAngularSpeed/d\TimeInstant = (\CommandedJointTorque - \WheelRadius \TractiveForce)/\WheelInertia > 0\)). The resulting slip can reduce lateral authority or disturb yaw, a cyber-physical disconnect that cannot be detected by directional agreement or cosine similarity metrics that evaluate only vector angles while ignoring control magnitude.

Grasp mechanics: Friction cones and force closure

The nonadhesive contact model above lets a contact push but never pull. In the parallel-jaw hold considered here, friction supports the object against downward motion. Coulomb’s law bounds the tangential force \(\FingerTangentialForce\) a contact can carry by the normal force \(\FingerNormalForce\) pressing it, \(\|\FingerTangentialForce\| \le \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \FingerNormalForce\), where \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) is the friction coefficient between finger and object. Every admissible contact force therefore lies inside a friction cone about the contact normal, with half-angle \(\arctan \CoulombFrictionCoefficient\).

A parallel-jaw hold shows the consequence with nothing beyond Newton’s second law. Two fingers squeeze an object of mass \(\Mass\) on opposite vertical faces, each with normal force \(\FingerNormalForce\). Friction at the two contacts must carry the weight and any upward acceleration \(\GraspVerticalAcceleration\) the arm imposes while it moves: \[ 2\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \FingerNormalForce \ge \Mass\,(\Gravity + \GraspVerticalAcceleration) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \FingerNormalForce \ge \frac{\Mass\,(\Gravity + \GraspVerticalAcceleration)}{2\CoulombFrictionCoefficient} \tag{1}\] The required squeeze grows with the arm’s acceleration and inversely with friction: halving \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) doubles it. The object sets the other bound: a thin-walled cup or a ripe fruit deforms or breaks above some normal force. A grasp is feasible only when the interval between the two bounds is nonempty, and that interval is the band a guarded-contact check \(\GraspForceFloor \le \FingerNormalForce \le \GraspForceCeiling\) enforces (Grounded Affordances). In this two-finger model, the contact-force magnitude in that check denotes the normal squeeze at each finger, measured or conservatively inferred under qualified contact geometry. A generic force-vector norm or the net object wrench cannot substitute for that squeeze. Each term is a measurement the grasp needs: the object’s mass, the friction coefficient of this finger material on this surface, the planned peak acceleration, and the crush force. Horizontal acceleration and the moment of an off-center load add terms of the same form.

The general statement collects every contact into one linear map. A force \(\ContactPointForce_i\) applied at contact \(\ContactPointIndex\), located at \(\ContactPointOffset_i\) from the object’s reference point, exerts on the object the wrench formed by the force and its moment \(\ContactPointOffset_i \times \ContactPointForce_i\). Stacking all contact forces into \(\StackedContactForces\) gives the grasp map \(\GraspMap\), with object wrench \(\ObjectWrench = \GraspMap\StackedContactForces\) (the same bold letter as the Kalman gain, but a different matrix). A grasp has force closure when every external wrench \(\ExternalObjectWrench\) on the object can be balanced, \(\GraspMap\StackedContactForces = -\ExternalObjectWrench\), by contact forces that all lie inside their friction cones (Lynch and Park 2017). Approximating each cone by a few edge vectors makes the test finite: force closure holds when the origin lies strictly inside the convex hull of the primitive wrenches those edges generate. For two fingers in a plane, Nguyen’s condition is its geometric form: the segment joining the contact points must lie inside both friction cones (Nguyen 1988). In three dimensions, two point contacts cannot resist a torque about the line joining them; real fingertips resist it through torsional friction over a finite contact patch, the soft-finger contact model. Ferrari and Canny’s quality measure, the radius of the largest origin-centered ball inside the hull, turns the yes-or-no test into the smallest disturbance the grasp is guaranteed to resist per unit of contact force (Ferrari and Canny 1992).

Nguyen, Van-Duc. 1988. “Constructing Force-Closure Grasps.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 7 (3): 3–16. https://doi.org/10.1177/027836498800700301.
Ferrari, Carlo, and John Canny. 1992. “Planning Optimal Grasps.” Proceedings of the IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 2290–95. https://doi.org/10.1109/ROBOT.1992.219918.

Force closure is qualitative. It says a balancing set of forces exists, not that the gripper can produce them within its force limit or without crushing the object. The magnitude check of equation 1 therefore remains necessary for every grasp the machine admits.

Continuous Covariance Propagation and Observability

When sensors suffer line-of-sight dropouts or environmental occlusion, internal state estimators must project the physical state forward in time. Quantifying how rapidly estimation uncertainty grows across that blind interval requires continuous error-covariance dynamics and observability analysis. The discrete Kalman filter and its extended form, which the machine runs on dated measurements, are developed in Estimating State from Dated Measurements. The consistency tests that decide whether a filter’s reported covariance can be trusted are in Testing whether the covariance is honest. This section treats the continuous-time limit that governs uncertainty growth between measurements.

Continuous Lyapunov and Riccati covariance dynamics

To track how state-estimation uncertainty grows from process noise and contracts under sensor updates, consider a continuous-time linear dynamic system governed by: \[ \PlantStateRate(\TimeInstant) = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) + \StateSpaceInputMatrix \ControlInput(\TimeInstant) + \ProcessNoiseVector(\TimeInstant) \] \[ \MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant) = \MeasurementMatrix \MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) + \MeasurementNoiseVector(\TimeInstant) \] For the continuous white-noise model, \(\ProcessNoiseIntensity\) and \(\ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity\) are process and measurement noise intensities, with covariance kernels \(\mathbb{E}[\ProcessNoiseVector(\TimeInstant)\ProcessNoiseVector(\PairedTimeInstant)^\top]=\ProcessNoiseIntensity\UnitImpulseFunction(\TimeInstant-\PairedTimeInstant)\) and \(\mathbb{E}[\MeasurementNoiseVector(\TimeInstant)\MeasurementNoiseVector(\PairedTimeInstant)^\top]=\ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity\UnitImpulseFunction(\TimeInstant-\PairedTimeInstant)\). The model assumes suitable independence and \(\ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity\succ0\). Measurement-noise intensity carries products of measurement-component units times seconds; per-sample measurement covariance carries products of measurement-component units.

Under an optimal continuous-time estimator (the Kalman-Bucy filter (Kalman 1960)), the error covariance matrix \(\EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) = \mathbb{E}[(\MechanicalPlantState - \EstimatedPlantState)(\MechanicalPlantState - \EstimatedPlantState)^\top]\) evolves according to the continuous matrix differential Riccati equation:6 \[ \ErrorCovarianceDerivative(\TimeInstant) = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) + \EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top + \ProcessNoiseIntensity - \EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) \MeasurementMatrix^\top \ContinuousMeasurementNoiseIntensity^{-1} \MeasurementMatrix \EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) \]

Kalman, Rudolph Emil. 1960. “A New Approach to Linear Filtering and Prediction Problems.” Journal of Basic Engineering 82 (1): 35–45. https://doi.org/10.1115/1.3662552.

6 Continuous Matrix Riccati Equation: The measurement term reduces modeled covariance when valid measurements arrive. During occlusion, propagation follows the plant and process-noise model. Its growth may be bounded, linear, polynomial, or exponential depending on the dynamics and noise; the estimator must compare the resulting bound with the task tolerance.

When direct sensing is occluded or dropped (\(\MeasurementMatrix = \ZeroMatrix\)), the measurement correction term vanishes. The covariance growth collapses to the open-loop continuous Lyapunov differential equation: \[ \ErrorCovarianceDerivative(\TimeInstant) = \StateSpaceSystemMatrix \EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) + \EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) \StateSpaceSystemMatrix^\top + \ProcessNoiseIntensity \] The solution propagates initial uncertainty and accumulated \(\ProcessNoiseIntensity\) through \(e^{\StateSpaceSystemMatrix{}\TimeInstant}\). Unstable modes can grow exponentially; a random walk with \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix=0\) grows linearly in covariance. A state-memory record should carry the calibrated growth model and expire tracking permission before its declared error bound crosses the physical tolerance. Covariance alone is not a hard error envelope unless its tail and model assumptions are justified.

Closed-form double-integrator covariance expansion

Consider a one-dimensional double-integrator kinematic system modeling position \(\DoubleIntegratorPosition(\TimeInstant)\) and velocity \(\DoubleIntegratorVelocity(\TimeInstant)\) under unmeasured stochastic acceleration noise \(\AccelerationWhiteNoise(\TimeInstant)\): \[ \begin{bmatrix} \DoubleIntegratorPositionDerivative(\TimeInstant) \\ \DoubleIntegratorVelocityDerivative(\TimeInstant) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \DoubleIntegratorPosition(\TimeInstant) \\ \DoubleIntegratorVelocity(\TimeInstant) \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} \AccelerationWhiteNoise(\TimeInstant) \] where \(\AccelerationWhiteNoise(\TimeInstant)\) is zero-mean continuous white process noise with continuous spectral power intensity \(\AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity > 0\), such that \(\mathbb{E}[\AccelerationWhiteNoise(\TimeInstant) \AccelerationWhiteNoise(\PairedTimeInstant)] = \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \UnitImpulseFunction(\TimeInstant - \PairedTimeInstant)\). Here, \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}\) and the continuous process noise matrix is \(\ProcessNoiseIntensity = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \end{bmatrix}\). This intensity is not a per-sample acceleration variance. For position in meters and time in seconds, its units are square meters per cubic second, so multiplying it by elapsed time cubed gives positional variance in square meters.

Because \(\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^2=\ZeroMatrix\), the matrix exponential terminates after its linear term. The state transition is: \[ \ContinuousStateTransitionMatrix(\TimeInstant) = \IdentityMatrix + \StateSpaceSystemMatrix{}\TimeInstant = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \TimeInstant \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \] During a blind occlusion window \(\TimeInstant \in [0, \FiniteChange \TimeInstant]\) with no measurement updates (\(\MeasurementMatrix = \ZeroMatrix\)), the total state covariance \(\EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant)\) evolves as the sum of unforced homogeneous propagation and accumulated process diffusion: \[ \EstimationErrorCovariance(\TimeInstant) = \ContinuousStateTransitionMatrix(\TimeInstant) \EstimationErrorCovariance_0 \ContinuousStateTransitionMatrix(\TimeInstant)^\top + \int_0^\TimeInstant \ContinuousStateTransitionMatrix(\TimeInstant - \ConvolutionTimeInstant) \ProcessNoiseIntensity \ContinuousStateTransitionMatrix(\TimeInstant - \ConvolutionTimeInstant)^\top d\ConvolutionTimeInstant \] Let the initial state covariance at the instant of sensor loss (\(\TimeInstant = 0\)) be: \[ \EstimationErrorCovariance_0 = \begin{bmatrix} \InitialPositionStandardDeviation^2 & \InitialPositionVelocityCovariance \\ \InitialPositionVelocityCovariance & \InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2 \end{bmatrix} \] The homogeneous propagation term evaluates to: \[ \ContinuousStateTransitionMatrix(\TimeInstant) \EstimationErrorCovariance_0 \ContinuousStateTransitionMatrix(\TimeInstant)^\top = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \TimeInstant \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \InitialPositionStandardDeviation^2 & \InitialPositionVelocityCovariance \\ \InitialPositionVelocityCovariance & \InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ \TimeInstant & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \InitialPositionStandardDeviation^2 + 2\InitialPositionVelocityCovariance \TimeInstant + \InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2 \TimeInstant^2 & \InitialPositionVelocityCovariance + \InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2 \TimeInstant \\ \InitialPositionVelocityCovariance + \InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2 \TimeInstant & \InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2 \end{bmatrix} \] The accumulated stochastic diffusion term integrates across elapsed time: \[ \AccumulatedProcessNoiseCovariance(\TimeInstant) = \int_0^\TimeInstant \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \TimeInstant - \ConvolutionTimeInstant \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ \TimeInstant - \ConvolutionTimeInstant & 1 \end{bmatrix} d\ConvolutionTimeInstant = \int_0^\TimeInstant \begin{bmatrix} \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity (\TimeInstant - \ConvolutionTimeInstant)^2 & \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity (\TimeInstant - \ConvolutionTimeInstant) \\ \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity (\TimeInstant - \ConvolutionTimeInstant) & \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \end{bmatrix} d\ConvolutionTimeInstant = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{1}{3} \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \TimeInstant^3 & \frac{1}{2} \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \TimeInstant^2 \\ \frac{1}{2} \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \TimeInstant^2 & \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \TimeInstant \end{bmatrix} \] Summing the homogeneous and stochastic contributions yields the closed-form positional variance \(\PropagatedPositionStandardDeviation^2(\TimeInstant) = \EstimationErrorCovariance_{11}(\TimeInstant)\): \[ \PropagatedPositionStandardDeviation^2(\TimeInstant) = \InitialPositionStandardDeviation^2 + 2 \InitialPositionVelocityCovariance \TimeInstant + \InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2 \TimeInstant^2 + \frac{1}{3} \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \TimeInstant^3 \] This derivation explains the distinct physical error regimes during sensor occlusion. Initial velocity uncertainty \(\InitialVelocityStandardDeviation^2\) induces quadratic variance growth in time (\(\TimeInstant^2\)), while continuous stochastic disturbances \(\AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity\) (such as air currents or unmodeled drivetrain chatter) inject cubic uncertainty growth (\(\frac{1}{3} \AccelerationNoiseSpectralDensity \TimeInstant^3\)). In long blind intervals, the cubic term dominates, rapidly exhausting safety margins unless motion is derated or halted.

Observability rank and Lie derivative extensions

Measuring only a mobile base’s wheel speeds tells an estimator how fast the base is moving, but not where it started in the aisle: two trajectories shifted by \(0.5\text{ m}\) produce identical wheel-speed traces. In state estimation and machine learning, observability is the mathematical dual of reachability: whereas reachability asks whether latent internal states can be steered by control inputs, observability asks whether latent states can be reconstructed from external sensor observations \(\MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant)\).

In modern deep learning (such as world models, state-space models, and latent representation learning), neural encoders \(\EncodedLatentState = \LatentStateEncoder(\MeasurementVector)\) attempt to reconstruct underlying states from sensory observations. Observability establishes the information-theoretic limit of this reconstruction: if a dynamical system is unobservable, the mapping from physical state trajectories to sensor observations is strictly non-injective. In that case, distinct physical states can produce mathematically indistinguishable observation sequences. No neural network, regardless of parameter count or training data, can reconstruct an unobservable state without additional information. Active excitation can help some nonlinear systems, but a known input cannot repair the unobservable subspace of a fixed LTI model.

A linear time-invariant system \((\StateSpaceSystemMatrix, \MeasurementMatrix)\) is observable on \([0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]\) if and only if the Kalman observability matrix has full column rank \(\StateDimension\): \[ \ObservabilityMatrix = \begin{bmatrix} \MeasurementMatrix \\ \MeasurementMatrix\StateSpaceSystemMatrix \\ \vdots \\ \MeasurementMatrix\StateSpaceSystemMatrix^{n-1} \end{bmatrix}, \quad \operatorname{rank}(\ObservabilityMatrix) = \StateDimension \] If \(\operatorname{rank}(\ObservabilityMatrix) < \StateDimension\), the unobservable subspace \(\ker(\ObservabilityMatrix)\) contains initial state trajectories that produce identical output measurements. No linear or nonlinear estimator can distinguish between initial states that differ along \(\ker(\ObservabilityMatrix)\).

For a smooth autonomous nonlinear system \(\PlantStateRate = \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\), \(\MeasurementVector = \NonlinearOutputFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) where \(\NonlinearOutputFunction = [\OutputComponentFunction_1(\MechanicalPlantState), \dots, \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputDimension}(\MechanicalPlantState)]^\top\), the Hermann-Krener observability criterion (Hermann and Krener 1977) generalizes the Kalman rank test via successive directional derivatives along system dynamics. Differentiating the measurement output equation \(\MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant) = \NonlinearOutputFunction(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant))\) with respect to continuous time yields: \[ \MeasurementVectorDerivative(\TimeInstant) = \frac{\partial \NonlinearOutputFunction}{\partial \MechanicalPlantState} \PlantStateRate = \frac{\partial \NonlinearOutputFunction}{\partial \MechanicalPlantState} \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) = \DriftLieDerivativeOperator \NonlinearOutputFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \] Differentiating repeatedly produces the sequence of Lie derivatives, which are directional derivatives of the measurement channels along the unforced drift vector field \(\ControlAffineDriftField\): \[ \MeasurementVector^{(\LieDerivativeOrder)}(\TimeInstant) = \DriftLieDerivativeOperator^\LieDerivativeOrder \NonlinearOutputFunction(\MechanicalPlantState), \quad \text{where } \DriftLieDerivativeOperator^\LieDerivativeOrder \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputIndex}(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla (\DriftLieDerivativeOperator^{\LieDerivativeOrder-1} \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputIndex}(\MechanicalPlantState)) \cdot \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) \] These successive directional derivatives reveal how temporal variations in output channels encode higher-order kinematic and dynamic states (such as recovering joint velocities and accelerations from encoder or camera position sequences) without requiring real-time numerical differentiation of noisy signals.

Hermann, Robert, and Arthur J. Krener. 1977. “Nonlinear Controllability and Observability.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 22 (5): 728–40.

7 Hermann-Krener Nonlinear Observability: Full rank of the observation codistribution is a local structural test, not a sensor-noise or finite-horizon accuracy bound. A missing state direction may require another sensor, an active maneuver with its own permission, or a restricted operating envelope.

The Hermann-Krener observation space \(\NonlinearObservationSpace\) is the real vector space of all repeated Lie derivatives \(\{\DriftLieDerivativeOperator^\LieDerivativeOrder \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputIndex}\}_{k \ge 0, 1 \le \OutputIndex \le \OutputDimension}\). Its gradient differentials span the observability codistribution: \[ \text{d}\NonlinearObservationSpace(\MechanicalPlantState) = \operatorname{span}\left\{ \text{d}\DriftLieDerivativeOperator^\LieDerivativeOrder \OutputComponentFunction_{\OutputIndex}(\MechanicalPlantState) \;\middle|\; \LieDerivativeOrder \ge 0, \; 1 \le \OutputIndex \le \OutputDimension \right\} \] where \(\text{d}\StateScalarFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = \frac{\partial \StateScalarFunction}{\partial \MechanicalPlantState}\). The system satisfies the nonlinear observability rank condition at \(\MechanicalPlantState_0\) if:7 \[ \operatorname{dim}(\text{d}\NonlinearObservationSpace(\MechanicalPlantState_0)) = \StateDimension \] When full rank \(\StateDimension\) is achieved, the directional gradients span the cotangent space \(\StateCotangentSpace \PlantStateSpace\), guaranteeing local weak observability: any infinitesimal deviation in latent state produces a distinguishable signature in the measurement sequence or its temporal derivatives.

Centroidal Dynamics and Floating-Base Control

Legged robots have an unactuated floating base as well as motorized joints. Internal joint motion can change the base pose, but only external forces change total linear momentum, and only external moments change total angular momentum. Ground contacts therefore determine which center-of-mass and momentum trajectories the robot can sustain against gravity.

Floating-base equations of motion

Let \(\GeneralizedCoordinates = [\FloatingBasePose^\top, \ActuatedJointCoordinates^\top]^\top \in \SpecialEuclideanGroup \times \mathbb{R}^\JointCount\) represent the generalized coordinates, where \(\FloatingBasePose \in \SpecialEuclideanGroup\) is the floating-base pose (position and orientation, 6 DoF) and \(\ActuatedJointCoordinates \in \mathbb{R}^\JointCount\) are the actuated joint angles. The total system possesses \(\JointCount + 6\) degrees of freedom. The displayed \(\GeneralizedVelocities\) and \(\GeneralizedAccelerations\) use a local coordinate convention; a global implementation must distinguish configuration coordinates from generalized velocities, particularly with quaternion orientation. The equations of motion are (Siciliano et al. 2009): \[ \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \ActuationSelectionMatrix^\top \JointTorque + \sum_{k=1}^{\ContactCount} {\ContactPointJacobian}_k(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \ContactPointForce_{k} \] The right-hand terms separate internal joint actuation from external contact support:

  • \(\ActuationSelectionMatrix = [\ZeroMatrix_{\JointCount \times 6}, \IdentityMatrix_{\JointCount \times \JointCount}] \in \mathbb{R}^{\JointCount \times (\JointCount+6)}\) is the actuation selection matrix separating the 6 unactuated floating-base coordinates from the \(\JointCount\) actuated joints.
  • \(\JointTorque \in \mathbb{R}^\JointCount\) is the commanded motor torque vector.
  • \({\ContactPointJacobian}_k(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \partial \CartesianPosition_k / \partial \GeneralizedCoordinates \in \mathbb{R}^{3 \times (\JointCount+6)}\) is the contact point Jacobian for point-contact force \(\ContactPointForce_{k} \in \mathbb{R}^3\).

Partitioning the equations of motion into floating-base (\(\Glyph{b}\)) and actuated joint (\(\Glyph{j}\)) coordinates highlights the underactuation of legged machines: \[ \begin{bmatrix} \FloatingBaseInertiaBlock(\GeneralizedCoordinates) & \BaseJointInertiaCoupling(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \\ \JointBaseInertiaCoupling(\GeneralizedCoordinates) & \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix_{jj}(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \FloatingBaseAccelerations \\ \ActuatedJointAccelerations \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} \FloatingBaseBiasForces(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \\ \ActuatedJointBiasForces(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \ZeroMatrix \\ \JointTorque \end{bmatrix} + \sum_{k=1}^{\ContactCount} \begin{bmatrix} \ContactJacobianBaseBlock^\top \\ \ContactJacobianJointBlock^\top \end{bmatrix} \ContactPointForce_{k} \] where \(\GeneralizedBiasForces(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) = \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\). The top 6 rows have identically zero direct motor authority (\(\ActuationSelectionBaseBlock^\top \JointTorque = \ZeroMatrix\)). The base equation still contains gravity and coupling to joint acceleration, so the base can accelerate even in flight: \[ \FloatingBaseInertiaBlock(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \FloatingBaseAccelerations + \BaseJointInertiaCoupling(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ActuatedJointAccelerations + \FloatingBaseBiasForces(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) = \sum_{k=1}^{\ContactCount} \ContactJacobianBaseBlock(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \ContactPointForce_{k} \]

Centroidal momentum dynamics

Projecting the multi-body floating-base dynamics onto the robot’s center of mass \(\MassCenterPosition \in \mathbb{R}^3\) yields the Newton-Euler centroidal momentum equations (Lynch and Park 2017): \[ \CentroidalLinearMomentumDerivative = \Mass \MassCenterAcceleration = \sum_{k=1}^{\ContactCount} \ContactPointForce_{k} + \Mass \GravityVector \] \[ \CentroidalAngularMomentumDerivative = \sum_{k=1}^{\ContactCount}\left[(\CartesianPosition_k - \MassCenterPosition) \times \ContactPointForce_{k} + \ContactPointTorque_k\right] \] where \(\CentroidalLinearMomentum\) is linear momentum, \(\CentroidalAngularMomentum\) is angular momentum about the CoM, \(\Mass\) is the total robot mass, \(\GravityVector\) is the gravitational acceleration vector, and \((\CartesianPosition_k, \ContactPointForce_{k}, \ContactPointTorque_k)\) are the position, force, and moment at each active contact patch.8

8 Centroidal Momentum Matrix: Centroidal dynamics summarize the external wrench needed for a proposed CoM and angular-momentum change. A controller still needs feasible contacts and actuator torque; omitting angular momentum can mispredict the required contact moments.

The total centroidal momentum \(\CentroidalMomentum = [\CentroidalLinearMomentum^\top, \CentroidalAngularMomentum^\top]^\top \in \mathbb{R}^6\) linearly maps from generalized velocities through the Centroidal Momentum Matrix (CMM) \(\CentroidalMomentumMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \in \mathbb{R}^{6 \times (\JointCount+6)}\) (Lynch and Park 2017): \[ \CentroidalMomentum = \CentroidalMomentumMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedVelocities \] Differentiating with respect to time relates generalized accelerations to external contact wrenches: \[ \CentroidalMomentumRate = \CentroidalMomentumMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \CentroidalMomentumMatrixDerivative(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities = \sum_{k=1}^{\ContactCount} \ContactWrenchTransform \ContactWrench_k + \begin{bmatrix} \Mass \GravityVector \\ \ZeroMatrix \end{bmatrix} \] where \(\ContactWrenchTransform\) transforms contact wrench \(\ContactWrench_k = [\ContactPointForce_{k}^\top, \ContactPointTorque_k^\top]^\top\) to the center of mass frame.

Physical contact feasibility constraints

For nonadhesive support contacts with finite friction, admissible contact wrenches must satisfy three physical invariants:

  1. Unilateral Normal Force: Feet can only push against the ground: \[ \NormalContactForce = \ContactPointForce_{k}^\top \ContactNormal_k \ge 0 \]
  2. Coulomb Friction Cone: Tangential shear forces cannot exceed normal traction: \[ \| \TangentialContactShear \| = \| \ContactPointForce_{k} - \NormalContactForce \ContactNormal_k \| \le \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \NormalContactForce \]
  3. Support condition for coplanar contacts: Under flat-ground assumptions, the Zero Moment Point (ZMP) should remain inside the effective support polygon with a case-specific inset:9 \[ \ZeroMomentPoint\in\SupportPolygon,\qquad \SupportPolygon=\operatorname{Conv}\!\left(\bigcup_k\ContactPatchRegion_k\right), \] where \(\ContactPatchRegion_k\) is each active contact patch projected onto that plane.

9 Zero Moment Point (ZMP): ZMP is useful for a specified coplanar support model. Edge contact, friction loss, and multi-height supports require more general wrench feasibility checks; crossing a modeled polygon edge does not by itself predict a particular fall.

Hierarchical whole-body control quadratic program

In floating-base humanoid and legged systems, high-level policies (such as learned vision-language-action models or action chunking policies) propose desired kinematic behaviors: end-effector manipulation paths, torso orientation, gaze directions, or swing-foot trajectories. However, because the 6-DoF floating base is unactuated, these kinematic proposals cannot be executed directly by joint motors. The Hierarchical Whole-Body Control (WBC) Quadratic Program acts as a real-time multi-task constrained optimization projection layer. It projects competing operational-space task accelerations \(\DesiredTaskSpaceAcceleration_i\) through a prioritized optimization hierarchy while strictly enforcing rigid-body Newton-Euler dynamics, friction cones, and actuator limits. In optimization and machine learning terms, Whole-Body Control solves the physical counterpart of multi-task learning with conflicting gradients:

Concept Multi-Task Deep Learning (PCGrad / MGDA) Hierarchical Whole-Body Control (WBC)
Task Specifications Loss functions \(\TaskSpecificLoss_1(\ModelParameters), \dots, \TaskSpecificLoss_{\TaskCount}(\ModelParameters)\) Operational-space tasks \(\TaskSpaceCoordinates_1 = \TaskKinematicMap_1(\GeneralizedCoordinates), \dots, \TaskSpaceCoordinates_{\TaskCount} = \TaskKinematicMap_{\TaskCount}(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\)
Shared Resource Parameter weight vector \(\ModelParameters \in \mathbb{R}^\Parameters\) Generalized joint accelerations \(\GeneralizedAccelerations \in \mathbb{R}^{\JointCount+6}\) and torques \(\JointTorque\)
Task Conflict Negative gradient inner product \(\TaskLossGradient_i^\top \TaskLossGradient_j < 0\) Task accelerations that cannot be achieved simultaneously
Decoupling Rule Projecting gradients onto tangent hyperplanes (PCGrad) Null-space projection operator \(\NullSpaceProjector_i = \IdentityMatrix - \TaskJacobianPseudoinverse_i \TaskJacobian_i\)
Priority Hierarchy Heuristic loss weighting or GradNorm tuning Cascaded optimization preserving higher-priority optima
Feasibility Enforcer Parameter norm clipping / weight decay Hard KKT constraints: dynamics, friction cones, torque saturation

Operational-space tasks and analytical null-space projection

Let task \(\ControlTaskIndex\) be defined by a kinematics mapping \(\TaskSpaceCoordinates_i = \TaskKinematicMap_i(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \in \mathbb{R}^{\TaskSpaceDimension_i}\) (e.g., end-effector Cartesian pose, center of mass position, or torso orientation). Differentiating twice yields the task acceleration: \[ \TaskSpaceAcceleration_i = \TaskJacobian_i(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \TaskJacobianDerivative_i(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities \] A tracking controller generates desired task acceleration via proportional-derivative feedback: \[ \DesiredTaskSpaceAcceleration_i = \TaskSpaceReferenceAcceleration_i + \TaskProportionalGain (\TaskSpaceReference - \TaskSpaceCoordinates_i) + \TaskDerivativeGain (\TaskSpaceReferenceVelocity_i - \TaskSpaceVelocity_i) \] In complex humanoids, multiple tasks compete for limited degrees of freedom. Consider two prioritized tasks: Task 1 (highest priority, e.g., CoM balance and stance-foot ground contact) and Task 2 (secondary priority, e.g., end-effector trajectory tracking).

To ensure Task 2 never compromises Task 1, analytical Whole-Body Control projects Task 2 into the kinematic null space of Task 1. The unconstrained acceleration for Task 1 is: \[ \GeneralizedAccelerations_1^* = \TaskJacobianPseudoinverse_1 \left( \DesiredTaskSpaceAcceleration_1 - \TaskJacobianDerivative_1 \GeneralizedVelocities \right) \] Assuming \(\TaskJacobian_1\) has full row rank, \(\TaskJacobianPseudoinverse_1 = \TaskJacobian_1^\top (\TaskJacobian_1 \TaskJacobian_1^\top)^{-1}\) is the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse. The null-space projection matrix of Task 1 is: \[ \NullSpaceProjector_1 = \IdentityMatrix - \TaskJacobianPseudoinverse_1 \TaskJacobian_1 \] The projector satisfies the algebraic property: \[ \TaskJacobian_1 \NullSpaceProjector_1 = \TaskJacobian_1 \left( \IdentityMatrix - \TaskJacobianPseudoinverse_1 \TaskJacobian_1 \right) = \TaskJacobian_1 - \TaskJacobian_1 \TaskJacobianPseudoinverse_1 \TaskJacobian_1 = \ZeroMatrix \] Projecting the secondary task command \(\GeneralizedAccelerations_2\) through \(\NullSpaceProjector_1\): \[ \GeneralizedAccelerations^* = \GeneralizedAccelerations_1^* + \NullSpaceProjector_1 \GeneralizedAccelerations_2^* \] guarantees that Task 2 produces strictly zero acceleration in the direction of Task 1: \[ \TaskJacobian_1 \GeneralizedAccelerations^* = \TaskJacobian_1 \GeneralizedAccelerations_1^* + \TaskJacobian_1 \NullSpaceProjector_1 \GeneralizedAccelerations_2^* = \TaskJacobian_1 \GeneralizedAccelerations_1^* + \ZeroMatrix = \DesiredTaskSpaceAcceleration_1 - \TaskJacobianDerivative_1 \GeneralizedVelocities \] Task 2 operates exclusively within the redundant internal degrees of freedom left unconstrained by Task 1.

Embedded whole-body control quadratic program

While analytical pseudo-inverse projections provide elegant decoupling, they cannot enforce inequality constraints such as unilateral contact forces, friction cones, and motor torque limits. Real-time controller firmware resolves this by formulating Whole-Body Control as a convex Quadratic Program whose execution deadline is part of the controller design.

Collect the decision variables \(\WholeBodyDecisionVariables = [\GeneralizedAccelerations^\top, \StackedContactForces^\top, \JointTorque^\top]^\top\), where \(\GeneralizedAccelerations \in \mathbb{R}^{\JointCount+6}\) is generalized acceleration, \(\StackedContactForces \in \mathbb{R}^{3\ContactCount}\) collects contact forces, and \(\JointTorque \in \mathbb{R}^\JointCount\) is motor torque. A weighted task QP for a fixed set of sticking contacts then solves: \[ \begin{aligned} \min_{\GeneralizedAccelerations, \StackedContactForces, \JointTorque} \quad & \sum_{i=1}^\TaskCount \TaskWeight_i \| \TaskJacobian_i(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \TaskJacobianDerivative_i(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities - \DesiredTaskSpaceAcceleration_i \|^2 + \TorqueRegularizationWeight \| \JointTorque \|^2 + \ContactForceRegularizationWeight \| \StackedContactForces - \NominalContactForces \|^2 \\ \text{subject to:} \quad & \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \ActuationSelectionMatrix^\top \JointTorque + \sum_{k=1}^{\ContactCount} {\ContactPointJacobian}_k(\GeneralizedCoordinates)^\top \ContactPointForce_{k} \\ & {\ContactPointJacobian}_k(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations + {\ContactPointJacobianDerivative{}}_k(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \GeneralizedVelocities = \ZeroMatrix \quad \forall \ActiveContactIndex \in \{1,\dots,\ContactCount\} \quad \text{(stationary sticking contacts)} \\ & \FrictionPyramidMatrix_k \ContactPointForce_{k} \le \ZeroMatrix, \quad \NormalContactForce \ge 0 \quad \forall \ActiveContactIndex \in \{1, \dots, \ContactCount\} \quad \text{(friction pyramid)} \\ & -\JointTorqueLimits \le \JointTorque \le \JointTorqueLimits \\ & \GeneralizedAccelerationLowerBound(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \le \GeneralizedAccelerations \le \GeneralizedAccelerationUpperBound(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities) \end{aligned} \] In this multi-task optimization architecture:

  • Dynamic Feasibility: The equality constraint enforces the coupled multi-body floating-base equations of motion, including joint coupling, gravity, and the modeled external contact forces. The stance-contact equality prevents assigning a supporting force to a point that is simultaneously commanded to move away from the support.
  • Multi-Task Null-Space Hierarchy: Finite objective weights \(\TaskWeight_i\) express a preference (\(\TaskWeight_1 \gg \TaskWeight_2 \gg \dots \gg \TaskWeight_{\TaskCount}\)). High-priority tasks (maintaining center-of-mass balance, stance-foot grounding, and trunk stability) dominate lower-priority goals (arm trajectory tracking or joint posture regularization toward nominal rest configurations). In strict hierarchical QP formulations, tasks are solved lexicographically in cascaded QPs, fixing the optimal residual of higher-priority tasks as hard constraints for subsequent stages.
  • Physical Contact and Torque Protection: The linear inequalities enforce contact friction pyramids (\(\FrictionPyramidMatrix_k \ContactPointForce_{k} \le \ZeroMatrix\)) and motor saturation limits (\(|\JointTorque| \le \JointTorqueLimits\)), restricting the solution to the chosen friction and torque model. Actual slip, electrical current, and joint-limit containment also depend on model error, sampling, and actuator response.
  • The Physical AI Synthesis: Whole-Body Control serves as the real-time deterministic gatekeeper (The Per-Tick Gatekeeper). High-level learned policies (such as Vision-Language-Action foundation models or diffusion trajectory planners) propose uncertified task goals \(\TaskSpaceReference\), while the WBC QP checks modeled task, contact, and torque feasibility before admission.

Here \(\FrictionPyramidMatrix_k\) describes the chosen inner approximation to the measured friction cone. The joint-acceleration bounds \(\GeneralizedAccelerationLowerBound, \GeneralizedAccelerationUpperBound\) must be derived to preserve velocity and position limits over the integration step, with reserves for uncertainty. Finite task weights do not implement a strict hierarchy; cascaded QPs must preserve each higher-priority optimum as a constraint. The solve deadline must be validated with the selected contact count, solver, processor, friction estimate, and fallback. A feasible numerical solution does not itself prove that a foothold will support the load.

Passivity and Energy-Bounded Interaction

During teleoperation or stiff contact, transport delay can reduce feedback damping. Passivity evaluates a different property from tracking accuracy: whether a component can supply more energy through its ports than it has received or initially stored. This energy accounting is useful when connecting controllers to uncertain mechanical environments.

Energy storage and the passivity definition

At a mechanical interaction port, the inner product of effort and flow measures instantaneous power entering the plant. For a continuous-time dynamical system with state \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant)\), control input \(\PortInput(\TimeInstant)\) (an effort variable, such as force or torque \(\PortEffort\)), and measured output \(\PortOutput(\TimeInstant)\) (a flow variable, such as velocity \(\Velocity\)), the system is passive if there exists a continuously differentiable, non-negative storage function \(\StorageFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ge 0\) (representing the total internal physical energy stored in mass momentum and structural compliance) such that: \[\StorageFunctionDerivative(\MechanicalPlantState) \le \PortInput(\TimeInstant)^\top \PortOutput(\TimeInstant) - \InternalDissipationPower(\MechanicalPlantState) \tag{2}\] where \(\PortInput(\TimeInstant)^\top \PortOutput(\TimeInstant)\) represents instantaneous power injected into the system through the physical port, and \(\InternalDissipationPower(\MechanicalPlantState) \ge 0\) denotes internal energy dissipation (such as mechanical damping, viscous friction, or electrical resistance). Integrating equation 2 over any operational duration \([0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]\) yields the passivity energy inequality: \[\int_0^T \PortInput(\TimeInstant)^\top \PortOutput(\TimeInstant) \, d\TimeInstant \ge \StorageFunction(\MechanicalPlantState(\FiniteTimeHorizon)) - \StorageFunction(\MechanicalPlantState(0)) + \int_0^T \InternalDissipationPower(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant))\,d\TimeInstant \ge -\StorageFunction(\MechanicalPlantState(0)) \tag{3}\] Under equation 3 and the declared port orientation, the net physical energy extractable from this modeled component is strictly bounded by its initial stored energy \(\StorageFunction(\MechanicalPlantState(0))\). A loaded mechanism can still release enough stored energy to injure or damage; passivity is an energy-bounding certificate, not an instantaneous contact-force limit.

Under a well-posed, power-conserving feedback interconnection, passive components admit a combined storage inequality. Stability conclusions additionally depend on the storage bounds and on strictness or detectability conditions; passivity alone does not guarantee every internal state converges. A human operator, powered tool, or active environment also cannot be assumed passive merely because it is connected to the robot.

The transport delay destabilization hazard

An untreated effort/flow communication channel need not remain passive when delayed. A fixed pure delay \(\PureTransportDelay\) contributes a frequency-dependent phase lag \(\DelayPhaseLag=-\AngularFrequency \PureTransportDelay\) at angular frequency \(\AngularFrequency\). In teleoperation or compliant interaction across wireless networks, late force feedback can arrive out of phase with the operator’s current hand motion.

A negative instantaneous input power means the component is returning energy through its port. That is compatible with passivity when the energy was previously stored. The delayed link violates passivity only if its net delivered energy exceeds its incoming and initially stored energy under the declared port signs. Such excess can reduce damping or destabilize the coupled loop; the outcome depends on the controllers and mechanical environment.

Passivity preservation via wave variables

To prevent delay from injecting artificial energy into the teleoperation loop, Anderson and Spong (1989) introduced the wave variable transformation. Inspired by microwave transmission lines and scattering theory, wave variables map physical effort \(\PortEffort\) and flow \(\PortFlow\) into incoming and outgoing wave channels with characteristic wave impedance \(\WaveImpedance>0\): \[\LeaderForwardWave(\TimeInstant) = \frac{\LeaderPortEffort(\TimeInstant) + \WaveImpedance \LeaderPortFlow(\TimeInstant)}{\sqrt{2\WaveImpedance}}, \quad \LeaderReturnWave(\TimeInstant) = \frac{\LeaderPortEffort(\TimeInstant) - \WaveImpedance \LeaderPortFlow(\TimeInstant)}{\sqrt{2\WaveImpedance}} \tag{4}\] Computing instantaneous power from the wave transformation in equation 4 reveals: \[\LeaderPortPower(\TimeInstant) = \LeaderPortEffort(\TimeInstant)^\top \LeaderPortFlow(\TimeInstant) = \frac{1}{2} \|\LeaderForwardWave(\TimeInstant)\|^2 - \frac{1}{2} \|\LeaderReturnWave(\TimeInstant)\|^2\] In wave coordinates, power transmitted across the network port is the difference between incoming wave power \(\frac{1}{2}\|\LeaderForwardWave\|^2\) and reflected wave power \(\frac{1}{2}\|\LeaderReturnWave\|^2\). For an ideal transmission channel with communication delay \(\PureTransportDelay\), transmitted waves are delayed: \(\FollowerForwardWave(\TimeInstant) = \LeaderForwardWave(\TimeInstant - \PureTransportDelay)\) and \(\LeaderReturnWave(\TimeInstant) = \FollowerReturnWave(\TimeInstant - \PureTransportDelay)\). The total energy stored in the communication channel in flight satisfies: \[ \ChannelStoredEnergy(\TimeInstant) = \frac{1}{2} \int_{t - \PureTransportDelay}^\TimeInstant \|\LeaderForwardWave(\DelayWindowIntegrationTime)\|^2 \DerivativeOperator\DelayWindowIntegrationTime + \frac{1}{2} \int_{t - \PureTransportDelay}^\TimeInstant \|\FollowerReturnWave(\DelayWindowIntegrationTime)\|^2 \DerivativeOperator\DelayWindowIntegrationTime \ge 0 \] Because the channel stores transmitted energy without amplifying wave amplitudes, the scattered communication link is passive and lossless under the stated constant-delay model; the delay line itself supplies no strict dissipation. Variable delay, packet loss, sampling, and saturation require additional analysis. Even the ideal passive channel does not bound spatial tracking errors; kinodynamic watchdog leases (section 1.6.5) must be evaluated concurrently to guarantee spatial obstacle clearance.

Anderson, Robert J., and Mark W. Spong. 1989. “Bilateral Control of Teleoperators with Time Delay.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 34 (5): 494–501. https://doi.org/10.1109/9.24201.

Further Reading

For a deeper theoretical foundation in control theory, robotics, and dynamical systems, consult the following references:

  • Modern Robotics: Mechanics, Planning, and Control (Lynch and Park 2017) provides a rigorous treatment of kinematics and dynamics using screw theory and Lie groups.
  • Robotics: Modelling, Planning and Control (Siciliano et al. 2009) serves as a reference for multi-body robot dynamics, trajectory generation, and operational space control.
  • Feedback Systems: An Introduction for Scientists and Engineers (Åström and Murray 2021) establishes classical and state-space control theory, frequency-domain stability margins, and state estimation from first principles.
  • Applied Nonlinear Control (Slotine and Li 1991) provides a foundational treatment of nonlinear dynamical systems, Lyapunov stability certificates, and sliding mode control.
  • Control Barrier Functions: Theory and Applications (Ames et al. 2019) sets out the formulation of control barrier functions, safety-critical quadratic programming filters, and forward invariance.
  • A Time-Dependent Hamilton-Jacobi Formulation of Reachable Sets for Continuous Dynamic Games (Mitchell et al. 2005) establishes the level set method and viscosity solutions for continuous-state differential games and safety verification.
  • A Predictive Safety Filter for Learning-Based Control of Constrained Nonlinear Dynamical Systems (Wabersich and Zeilinger 2021) develops predictive safety filters and constrained MPC methods for safe learning on physical systems.
Lynch, Kevin M, and Frank C Park. 2017. Modern Robotics: Mechanics, Planning, and Control. Cambridge University Press.
Siciliano, Bruno, Lorenzo Sciavicco, Luigi Villani, and Giuseppe Oriolo. 2009. Robotics: Modelling, Planning and Control. Springer Science & Business Media.
Åström, Karl Johan, and Richard M Murray. 2021. Feedback Systems: An Introduction for Scientists and Engineers. Princeton university press.
Slotine, Jean-Jacques E, and Weiping Li. 1991. Applied Nonlinear Control. Prentice Hall.
Ames, Aaron D., Samuel Coogan, Magnus Egerstedt, Gennaro Notomista, Koushil Sreenath, and Paulo Tabuada. 2019. “Control Barrier Functions: Theory and Applications.” 2019 18th European Control Conference (ECC), 3420–31. https://doi.org/10.23919/ECC.2019.8796030.
Mitchell, Ian M., Alexandre M. Bayen, and Claire J. Tomlin. 2005. “A Time-Dependent Hamilton-Jacobi Formulation of Reachable Sets for Continuous Dynamic Games.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 50 (7): 947–57. https://doi.org/10.1109/TAC.2005.851439.
Wabersich, Kim P., and Melanie N. Zeilinger. 2021. “A Predictive Safety Filter for Learning-Based Control of Constrained Nonlinear Dynamical Systems.” Automatica 129: 109647.
